Now, brainstorming the **Enterprise Connector System**. First clarifying question:

When you say "take it to your own premise" — are you envisioning this as:

**(A)** The Raven platform deployed inside the client's infrastructure, connecting to their internal data sources (databases, file stores) that never leave their network — like a Palantir/Foundry model

**(B)** A hybrid where Raven runs in your cloud but reaches into the client's systems via secure tunnels/agents (like Fivetran's agent model)

**(C)** Both — enterprise gets the on-prem option, mid-market gets the cloud-with-agent option

This shapes the entire security model and connector architecture.

> DEVELOPER

Good call — that covers both ends of the market.

Next question: For the connector system, are you thinking of building a **generic connector framework** where you write connector adapters yourself (PostgreSQL connector, S3 connector, WordPress connector, etc.), or are you open to **embedding Airbyte as the data movement engine** and focusing Raven's effort on the post-ingestion layer (chunking, embedding, classification, knowledge graph routing)?

The tradeoff:

**(A)** Build your own connector framework — full control, lighter weight, but you're maintaining 20+ connectors long-term (schema changes, API versioning, auth flows per source)

**(B)** Embed Airbyte (open-source, MIT core) — 700+ connectors on day one, community maintains them, but adds a dependency and Airbyte's own resource footprint

**(C)** Hybrid — use Airbyte for the long tail of SaaS/cloud sources (Salesforce, HubSpot, Google Drive, etc.) but build native connectors for the high-value database/file sources (PostgreSQL, MySQL, S3, SharePoint) where you want tighter control and lower overhead

## Goals[​](#goals "Direct link to Goals")

The Airbyte Protocol describes a series of standard components and all the interactions between them in order to declare an ELT pipeline. All message passing across components is done via serialized JSON messages for inter-process communication.

This document describes the protocol as it exists in its CURRENT form. Stay tuned for an RFC on how the protocol will evolve.

This document is intended to contain ALL the rules of the Airbyte Protocol in one place. Anything not contained in this document is NOT part of the Protocol. At the time of writing, there is one known exception, which is the [Supported Data Types](/platform/understanding-airbyte/supported-data-types), which contains rules on data types that are part of the Protocol. That said, there are additional articles, e.g. [A Beginner's Guide to the Airbyte Catalog](/platform/understanding-airbyte/beginners-guide-to-catalog) that repackage the information in this document for different audiences.

## Key Concepts[​](#key-concepts "Direct link to Key Concepts")

There are 2 major components in the Airbyte Protocol: Source and Destination. These components are referred to as Actors. A source is an application that is described by a series of standard interfaces. This application extracts data from an underlying data store. A data store in this context refers to the tool where the data is actually stored. A data store includes: databases, APIs, anything that produces data, etc. For example, the Postgres Source is a Source that pulls from Postgres (which is a data store). A Destination is an application that is described by a series of standard interfaces that loads data into a data store.

The key primitives that the Protocol uses to describe data are Catalog, Configured Catalog, Stream, Configured Stream, and Field:

*   Stream - A Stream describes the schema of a resource and various metadata about how a user can interact with that resource. A resource in this context might refer to a database table, a resource in a REST API, or a data stream.
*   Field - A Field refers to a "column" in a Stream. In a database this would be a column; in a JSON object it is a field.
*   Catalog - A Catalog is a list of Streams that describes the data in the data store that a Source represents.

An Actor can advertise information about itself with an [Actor Specification](#actor-specification). One of the main pieces of information the specification shares is what information is needed to configure an Actor.

Each of these concepts is described in greater depth in their respective section.

## Airbyte source connector PostgreSQL CDC incremental sync

--- [Airbyte protocol docs] ---
### Actor Specification[​](#actor-specification "Direct link to Actor Specification")

…on[​](#actor-specification "Direct link to Actor Specification")

The specification allows the Actor to share information about itself.

The `connectionSpecification` is [JSONSchema](https://json-schema.org) that describes what information needs to the actor for it operate. e.g. If using a Postgres Source, the `ConnectorSpecification` would specify that a `hostname`, `port`, and `password` are required in order for the connector to function. This JSONSchema can be used to validate that the provided inputs are valid. e.g. If `port` is one of the fields and the JsonSchema in the `connectionSpecification` specifies that this field should be a number, if a user inputs "airbyte", they will receive an error. For connection specification, Airbyte adheres to JsonSchema validation rules. The Airbyte implementation of the Protocol is able to render this JSONSchema to produce a form for users to fill in the information for an Actor.

The specification also contains information about what features the Actor supports.

*   `protocol_version` describes which version of the protocol the Connector supports. The default value is "0.2.0".
*   `supported_destination_sync_modes` - describes which sync modes a destination is able to support. See [Sync Modes](#source-sync-modes).

`documentationUrl` and `changelogUrl` are optional fields that link to additional information about the connector.

The following are fields that still exist in the specification but are slated to be removed as they leak

--- [Airbyte protocol docs] ---
### Messages[​](#messages "Direct link to Messages") > AirbyteControlMessage[​](#airbytecontrolmessage "Direct link to AirbyteControlMessage")

### AirbyteControlMessage[​](#airbytecontrolmessage "Direct link to AirbyteControlMessage")

An `AirbyteControlMessage` is for connectors to signal to the Airbyte Platform or Orchestrator that an action with a side-effect should be taken. This means that the Orchestrator will likely be altering some stored data about the connector, connection, or sync.

```
AirbyteControlMessage:  type: object  additionalProperties: true  required:    - type    - emitted_at  properties:    type:      title: orchestrator type      description: "the type of orchestrator message"      type: string      enum:        - CONNECTOR_CONFIG    emitted_at:      description: "the time in ms that the message was emitted"      type: number    connectorConfig:      description: "connector config orchestrator message: the updated config for the platform to store for this connector"      "$ref": "#/definitions/AirbyteControlConnectorConfigMessage"
```

> AGENT

Concrete tradeoffs for the Postgres example:

**Airbyte Postgres connector:**
- Handles CDC (Change Data Capture) via WAL — detects inserts/updates/deletes automatically
- Handles incremental sync (only new/changed rows since last sync)
- Handles schema discovery (reads table structures, column types)
- Handles 50+ edge cases: SSL modes, SSH tunnels, logical replication slots, custom schemas
- **But:** each connector runs as a separate Docker container. Airbyte's platform itself needs ~2–4 GB RAM (scheduler, API server, temporal workflow engine, database). A sync job spins up source + destination containers temporarily.

**Native Raven Postgres connector:**
- You write a Go service: `sql.Open("postgres", dsn)` → query → chunk → embed. Maybe 200 lines of code.
- Lightweight — no extra containers, no Airbyte platform overhead
- **But:** you now own CDC, incremental sync, schema drift, connection pooling, SSH tunneling, SSL cert management. That's where the 200 lines becomes 2,000+ and keeps growing.

**The real question is: how many connectors will enterprise clients need?**

If it's 3–5 (Postgres, MySQL, S3, SharePoint), building native is cleaner and lighter. If it's 15+ (add Snowflake, BigQuery, Salesforce, HubSpot, Confluence, Google Drive, Notion, Jira, MongoDB, Oracle, MSSQL...), maintaining that is a full engineering team's job.

My recommendation is **(C) Hybrid** — but weighted differently than you might expect:

- **Build native connectors for databases** (Postgres, MySQL, ClickHouse) — these are simple `SELECT` queries over a Go SQL driver, your ingestion pipeline already speaks Go, and you avoid the Airbyte platform overhead for on-prem clients where resources matter
- **Use Airbyte for SaaS/cloud sources** (Salesforce, Google Drive, Confluence, SharePoint, HubSpot) — these APIs are auth nightmares (OAuth2 flows, refresh tokens, pagination, rate limits, API version deprecation) that you absolutely do not want to maintain

Does that split make sense for what you're envisioning? Or do you see the database connectors also needing CDC-level complexity (real-time sync as client data changes)?

AvailableCoreAvailableStandardAvailablePlusAvailableProAvailableEnterprise FlexAvailableSelf-Managed Enterprise [Compare](https://airbyte.com/product/features)

The **Full Refresh** modes are the simplest methods that Airbyte uses to sync data, as they always retrieve all available information requested from the source, regardless of whether it has been synced before. This contrasts with [**Incremental sync**](/platform/using-airbyte/core-concepts/sync-modes/incremental-append), which does not sync data that has already been synced before.

In the **Overwrite** variant, new syncs will destroy all data in the existing destination table and then pull the new data in. Therefore, data that has been removed from the source after an old sync will be deleted in the destination table.

## Example Behavior[​](#example-behavior "Direct link to Example Behavior")

On the nth sync of a full refresh connection:

## _Replace_ existing data with new data. The connection does not create any new tables.[​](#replace-existing-data-with-new-data-the-connection-does-not-create-any-new-tables "Direct link to replace-existing-data-with-new-data-the-connection-does-not-create-any-new-tables")

## Destination-specific mechanism for full refresh[​](#destination-specific-mechanism-for-full-refresh "Direct link to Destination-specific mechanism for full refresh")

The mechanism by which a destination connector accomplishes the full refresh will vary wildly from destination to destination. For our certified database and data warehouse destinations, we will be recreating the final table each sync. This allows us leave the previous sync's data viewable by writing to a "final-table-tmp" location as the sync is running, and at the end dropping the old "final" table, and renaming the new one into place. That said, this may not possible for all destinations, and we may need to erase the existing data at the start of each full-refresh sync.

Typing and deduping | Airbyte Docs

AvailableCoreAvailableStandardAvailablePlusAvailableProAvailableEnterprise FlexAvailableSelf-Managed Enterprise [Compare](https://airbyte.com/product/features)

warning

**Typing and Deduping is currently being phased out in favor of [Direct-Load](/platform/using-airbyte/core-concepts/direct-load-tables).**

With Direct Loads, there is no intermediate table result containing the raw JSON blob. Instead, top-level fields are typed during time of insert, and there is only a single destination table maintained per stream.

This page refers to new functionality added by [Destinations V2](/release_notes/upgrading_to_destinations_v2/). Typing and deduping is the default method of transforming datasets within data warehouse and database destinations after they've been replicated. Please check each destination to learn if Typing and Deduping is supported.

## What is Destinations V2?[​](#what-is-destinations-v2 "Direct link to What is Destinations V2?")

[Airbyte Destinations V2](/release_notes/upgrading_to_destinations_v2) provide:

*   One-to-one table mapping: Data in one stream will always be mapped to one table in your data warehouse. No more sub-tables.
*   Improved per-row error/change handling with `_airbyte_meta`: Airbyte will now populate typing changes in the `_airbyte_meta` column instead of failing your sync. You can query these results to audit misformatted or unexpected data.
*   Internal Airbyte tables in the `airbyte_internal` schema: Airbyte will now generate all raw tables in the `airbyte_internal` schema. We no longer clutter your desired schema with raw data tables.
*   Incremental delivery for large syncs: Data will be incrementally delivered to your final tables when possible. No more waiting hours to see the first rows in your destination table.

## `_airbyte_meta` Changes[​](#_airbyte_meta-changes "Direct link to _airbyte_meta-changes")

"Per-row change handling" is a new paradigm for Airbyte which provides greater flexibility for our users. Airbyte now separates `data-moving problems` from `data-content problems`. Prior to Destinations V2, both types of errors were handled the same way: by failing the sync. Now, a failing sync means that Airbyte could not _move_ all of your data. You can query the `_airbyte_meta` column to see which rows failed for _content_ reasons, and why. This is a more flexible approach, as you can now decide how to handle rows with errors/changes on a case-by-case basis.

tip

When using data downstream from Airbyte, we generally recommend you only include rows which do not have an change, e.g:

```
-- postgres syntaxSELECT COUNT(*) FROM _table_ WHERE json_array_length(_airbyte_meta ->> changes) = 0
```

The types of changes which will be stored in `_airbyte_meta.changes`

> AGENT

Here's the honest picture on Airbyte's data integrity and speed tradeoffs:

**For existing data (initial sync):** Airbyte does a **Full Refresh** — it `SELECT *` from the source tables you've configured and streams it all to the destination. This works reliably. No data corruption risk here; it's a straightforward read.

**The real tradeoffs worth knowing about:**

### 1. Type coercion / data content issues
Airbyte has a "typing and deduping" layer. When it reads a column from Postgres that's, say, `JSONB` or `NUMERIC(38,18)`, it has to map that to its internal type system. Mismatches don't fail the sync — they get logged in an `_airbyte_meta.changes` column per row. This means **you won't lose data, but some rows may arrive with type warnings** that your ingestion pipeline needs to handle. For Raven this is actually fine — you're chunking text for embeddings, not doing financial arithmetic on decimal precision.

### 2. Deduplication on re-syncs
If a sync fails midway and retries, or if CDC misses a WAL event (e.g., WAL retention expired), Airbyte falls back to full refresh. The dedup layer handles this, but there's a window where **your destination could temporarily have duplicates** until dedup runs. For Raven, this means your chunking pipeline needs to be **idempotent** — if the same row arrives twice, it shouldn't create duplicate embeddings. A simple `ON CONFLICT (source_id, chunk_hash) DO NOTHING` solves this.

### 3. Schema drift
If the client alters their source table (adds/removes/renames columns) between syncs, Airbyte detects this and can propagate, but your downstream pipeline needs to handle it. A column renamed from `description` to `content` means your chunking config that targets `description` would silently get empty values. This requires **schema mapping** in Raven's connector config — "which columns contain the text to chunk" — and validation on each sync.

### 4. Speed — initial bulk load
Airbyte is not the fastest bulk loader. For a 100M row Postgres table, a raw `COPY` command over the wire is faster than Airbyte's stream-and-serialize protocol. But this only matters on the initial full refresh. Once CDC takes over, incremental syncs are near-realtime (seconds to minutes). For Raven's use case — you're going to chunk and embed every row anyway, which is orders of magnitude slower than the data transfer itself — Airbyte's transfer speed is never the bottleneck.

### 5. WAL retention / CDC gap
If the client's Postgres WAL retention is too short (default is sometimes 1 hour) and a sync interval is longer, CDC can miss changes. Airbyte handles this by falling back to full refresh, but that's expensive on large tables. This is a **configuration documentation issue**, not a code issue — your enterprise deployment guide needs to specify minimum WAL retention per source type.

**Summary:**

| Risk | Severity for Raven | Mitigation |
|---|---|---|
| Type coercion edge cases | Low | You're extracting text, not doing math |
| Duplicate rows on retry | Medium | Idempotent chunking pipeline |
| Schema drift | Medium | Schema mapping config + validation per sync |
| Initial bulk speed | None | Embedding is the bottleneck, not transfer |
| CDC WAL gap | Low | Deployment docs specifying retention settings |

None of these are dealbreakers. The key architectural requirement from Raven's side is: **make the chunking/embedding pipeline idempotent and schema-aware**. With that, Airbyte is safe to rely on fully.

Does this address your concern? If so, the next brainstorming question is about the **classification layer** — how admins will map source data to knowledge bases.

48	**1. XDP Pre-filtering (Rate Limiting Offload)**
49	Drop or throttle malicious/excess traffic at the NIC — before the TCP stack, before Gin, before Valkey. Complements the existing application-layer Valkey rate limiter with a kernel-layer first line of defense. Most impactful under DDoS or high-concurrency scenarios on constrained hardware.

51	**2. Kernel-level Observability (Zero-agent Metrics)**
52	Collect CPU, memory, syscall, and network metrics via eBPF kprobes/tracepoints directly from the Go binary. Eliminates the need for a separate Prometheus node exporter or monitoring sidecar on the Pi — fits the minimal-footprint edge constraint.

54	**3. Security Audit Trail (Process + Syscall Monitoring)**
55	Trace `sys_execve` and socket calls to log every process spawn and outbound connection from the edge node. Supports GDPR/SOC2 audit requirements without a dedicated agent. Can detect anomalous behavior from the AI worker (Python) or other services.

Now — the next item you'd raised earlier was the **data classification system**. Specifically: how do we ensure that when data comes in through connectors, it's properly labeled so the knowledge graph knows "this data belongs to this org / this feature / this domain" — without requiring massive manual effort?

You mentioned data lakes/warehouses might already have labeling. Do you want to dig into the classification architecture now, or was there a different topic you wanted to address first?

> DEVELOPER

Yeah, so you didn't tell me: does ClickHouse plus Qubit make sense for a larger vector embedding as a knowledge database? I am not talking about ClickHouse plus Qubit vector path for observability. I am not sure what that entails, but I was talking about a replacement for Postgres and ParadeDB, perhaps. Whenever the data scale becomes that huge, I am sure it won't be happening anytime soon, but I just like to explore the possibility.

> AGENT

To be clear — yes, that's exactly what I meant in the roadmap. The "ClickHouse + QBit vector path" entry is about **replacing pgvector for knowledge base embeddings**, not observability. Let me answer directly.

**Yes, ClickHouse + QBit makes sense as your scaled vector store for RAG.** Here's the concrete comparison:

| | pgvector (now) | ClickHouse + QBit (at scale) |
|---|---|---|
| Index type | HNSW — must fit in RAM | No index needed — scans columnar storage directly |
| 1M chunks | ~8–12 GB RAM for index | ~740 MB peak memory during query |
| 10M chunks | ~80–120 GB RAM — needs dedicated server | Same architecture, scans partition only |
| 100M chunks | Not viable with HNSW | Viable — partition by org/kb, each partition is a small scan |
| Query precision | Fixed at index build time | Tunable per query (read more/fewer bit planes) |
| Pre-filtering | WHERE + vector search in same query — works but slow at scale | Partition pruning on org_id/kb_id, then QBit scans only that partition |
| BM25 keyword search | ParadeDB (excellent) | ClickHouse has full-text indexing too, or keep ParadeDB alongside |

The key insight for your use case: **every RAG query in Raven is scoped** — `WHERE org_id = X AND kb_id = Y`. With ClickHouse partitioned on those keys, QBit never scans the whole table. A 500K chunk knowledge base (one university course) scans in under 300ms. That's fast enough for RAG where the LLM response itself takes 1–3 seconds.

**The migration path is clean:**
- PostgreSQL stays as the relational store (users, orgs, workspaces, API keys, document metadata)
- ParadeDB stays for BM25 keyword search
- Only the **embeddings column** moves to ClickHouse + QBit
- Your Go retrieval service does two parallel queries: BM25 from Postgres, vector from ClickHouse, fuse with RRF — same pattern as today, just a different vector backend

**When to trigger the migration:** When any single tenant's total chunks exceed ~5M and you start seeing pgvector HNSW index builds taking 10+ minutes or RAM pressure on the Postgres instance. That's your signal.

The roadmap entry I wrote is exactly this — I'll fix the wording to make it unambiguous.

Introduction to object tagging | Snowflake Documentation  

# Introduction to object tagging[¶](#introduction-to-object-tagging "Link to this heading")

## What is a tag?[¶](#what-is-a-tag "Link to this heading")

A tag is a schema-level object that can be assigned to another Snowflake object. Users associate a tag with an arbitrary string value when assigning the tag to a Snowflake object. Snowflake stores the tag and its string value as a key-value pair. The tag must be unique for your schema, and the tag value is always a string.

The following are general characteristics of object tagging:

*   An object can have multiple tags at the same time. For more information, see [Tag quotas](#label-object-tagging-quota).
    
*   A single tag can be assigned to different object types at the same time (for example, a warehouse and a table simultaneously).
    
*   At the time of assignment, the tag string value can be duplicated or remain unique. For example, multiple tables can be assigned the `cost_center` tag and the tag can always have the string value `sales`. Alternatively, the string value could be different for each table (for example, `engineering`, `marketing`, and `finance`).
    

After defining the tags and assigning the tags to Snowflake objects, you can query them to monitor usage on objects and facilitate data governance operations, such as auditing and reporting.

### Highlights[¶](#highlights "Link to this heading")

Ease of use:

Define a tag once and apply it to as many different objects as desirable.

Tag inheritance:

Because tags are inherited, applying the tag to objects higher in the securable objects hierarchy results in the tag being applied to all child objects. For example, if a tag is set on a table, the tag will be inherited by all columns in that table.

Automatic propagation:

Configure a tag so it automatically propagates to target objects from a source object.

Consistent assignment with replication:

Snowflake replicates tags and their assignments within the primary database to the secondary database.

Centralized or decentralized management:

Tags support different management approaches to facilitate compliance with internal and external regulatory requirements.

In a centralized approach, you can create a `tag_admin` custom role that creates and applies tags to Snowflake objects.

In a decentralized approach, individual teams apply tags to Snowflake objects and the `tag_admin` custom role creates tags to ensure consistent tag naming.

## Using tags for data protection[¶](#using-tags-for-data-protection "Link to this heading")

Because tags can be assigned to tables, views, and columns, setting a tag and then querying the tag enables the discovery of a multitude of database objects and columns that contain sensitive information. Upon discovery, data stewards can determine how best to make that data available, such as selective filtering using [row access policies](../security-row-intro), or using [masking policies](../security-column-intro) to determine whether the data is tokenized, fu

…[truncated — use search() for full content]

# Introduction to labels

To help organize your BigQuery resources, you can add labels to your datasets, tables, reservations, and views. Labels are key-value pairs that you can attach to a resource. When you create BigQuery resources, labels are optional.

After labeling your resources, you can search for them based on label values. For example, you can use labels to group datasets by purpose, environment, department, and so on.

## What are labels?

A label is a key-value pair that you can assign to Google Cloud BigQuery resources. They help you organize these resources and manage your costs at scale, with the granularity you need. You can attach a label to each resource, then filter the resources based on their labels. Information about labels is forwarded to the billing system that lets you break down your billed charges by label. With built-in [billing reports](/billing/docs/how-to/reports), you can filter and group costs by resource labels. You can also use labels to query [billing data exports](/billing/docs/how-to/bq-examples).

## Requirements for labels

The labels applied to a resource must meet the following requirements:

*   Each resource can have up to 64 labels.
*   Each label must be a key-value pair.
*   Keys have a minimum length of 1 character and a maximum length of 63 characters, and cannot be empty. Values can be empty, and have a maximum length of 63 characters.
*   Keys and values can contain only lowercase letters, numeric characters, underscores, and dashes. All characters must use UTF-8 encoding, and international characters are allowed. Keys must start with a lowercase letter or international character.
*   The key portion of a label must be unique within a single resource. However, you can use the same key with multiple resources.

These limits apply to the key and value for each label, and to the individual Google Cloud res

# Data discovery and cataloging in AWS Glue

The AWS Glue Data Catalog is a centralized repository that stores metadata about your organization's data sets. It acts as an index to the location, schema, and runtime metrics of your data sources. The metadata is stored in metadata tables, where each table represents a single data store.

You can populate the Data Catalog using a crawler, which automatically scans your data sources and extracts metadata. A crawler can connect to data sources that are internal (AWS-based) and external to AWS.

For more information about the supported data sources, see [Supported data sources for crawling](./crawler-data-stores.html)

You can also create tables in the Data Catalog manually by defining the table structure, schema, and partitioning structure according to your specific requirements.

For more information about creating metadata tables manually, see [Defining metadata manually](./populate-dg-manual.html).

You can use the information in the Data Catalog to create and monitor your ETL jobs. The Data Catalog integrates with other AWS analytics services, providing a unified view of data sources making it easier to manage and analyze data.

*   Amazon Athena – Store and query table metadata in the Data Catalog for the Amazon S3 data using SQL.
    
*   AWS Lake Formation – Centrally define and manage fine-grained data access policies and audit data access.
    
*   Amazon EMR – Access data sources defined in the Data Catalog for big data processing.
    
*   Amazon SageMaker AI – Quickly and confidently build, train, and deploy machine learning models.
    

###### Key features of the Data Catalog

The following are the key aspects of the Data Catalog.

Metadata repository

The Data Catalog acts as a central metadata repository, storing information about the location, schema, and properties of your data sources. This metadata is organized into databases and tables, similar to a traditional relational database catalog.

Automatic data discoverability

AWS Glue crawlers can automatically discover and catalog new or updated data sources, reducing the overhead of manual metadata management and ensuring that your Data Catalog remains up-to-date. By cataloging your data sources, the Data Catalog makes it easier for users and applications to discover and understand the available data assets within your organization, promoting data reuse and collaboration.

The Data Catalog supports a wide range of data sources, including Amazon S3, Amazon RDS, Amazon Redshift, Apache Hive, and more. It can automatically infer and store metadata from these sources using AWS Glue crawlers.

For more information see, [Using crawlers to populate the Data Catalog](./add-crawler.html).

Schema management

The Data Catalog automatically captures a

## Limitations and considerations[¶](#limitations-and-considerations "Link to this heading")

Future grants:

[Future grants](../../sql-reference/sql/grant-privilege.html#label-grant-privilege-schema-future-grants) of privileges on tags are not supported.

As a workaround, grant the APPLY TAG privilege to a custom role to allow that role to apply tags to another object.

Snowflake Native App:

Use caution when creating the setup script when tags exist in a versioned schema. For information, see [version schema considerations](../../developer-guide/native-apps/creating-setup-script.html#label-setup-script-versioned-schema-failures).

### Introduction to object tagging[¶](#introduction-to-object-tagging "Link to this heading") > What is a tag?[¶](#what-is-a-tag "Link to this heading")

## What is a tag?[¶](#what-is-a-tag "Link to this heading")

A tag is a schema-level object that can be assigned to another Snowflake object. Users associate a tag with an arbitrary string value when assigning the tag to a Snowflake object. Snowflake stores the tag and its string value as a key-value pair. The tag must be unique for your schema, and the tag value is always a string.

The following are general characteristics of object tagging:

*   An object can have multiple tags at the same time. For more information, see [Tag quotas](#label-object-tagging-quota).
    
*   A single tag can be assigned to different object types at the same time (for example, a warehouse and a table simultaneously).
    
*   At the time of assignment, the tag string value can be duplicated or remain unique. For example, multiple tables can be assigned the `cost_center` tag and the tag can always have the string value `sales`. Alternatively, the string value could be different for each table (for example, `engineering`, `marketing`, and `finance`).
    

After defining the tags and assigning the tags to Snowflake objects, you can query them to monitor usage on objects and facilitate data governance operations, such as auditing and reporting.

---

## Snowflake tag inheritance table column propagation

--- [Snowflake object tagging] ---
### Introduction to object tagging[¶](#introduction-to-object-tagging "Link to this heading") > What is a tag?[¶](#what-is-a-tag "Link to this heading") > Highlights[¶](#highlights "Link to this heading")

### Highlights[¶](#highlights "Link to this heading")

Ease of use:

Define a tag once and apply it to as many different objects as desirable.

Tag inheritance:

Because tags are inherited, applying the tag to objects higher in the securable objects hierarchy results in the tag being applied to all child objects. For example, if a tag is set on a table, the tag will be inherited by all columns in that table.

Automatic propagation:

Configure a tag so it automatically propagates to target objects from a source object.

Consistent assignment with replication:

Snowflake replicates tags and their assignments within the primary database to the secondary database.

Centralized or decentralized management:

Tags support different management approaches to facilitate compliance with internal and external regulatory requirements.

In a centralized approach, you can create a `tag_admin` custom role that creates and applies tags to Snowflake objects.

In a decentralized approach, individual teams apply tags to Snowflake objects and the `tag_admin` custom role creates tags to ensure consistent tag naming.

--- [Snowflake object tagging] ---
### Introduction to object tagging[¶](#introduction-to-object-tagging "Link to this heading") > How a tag gets associated with an object[¶](#how-a-tag-gets-associated-with-an-object "Link to this heading")

## How a tag gets associated with an object[¶](#how-a-tag-gets-associated-with-an-object "Link to this heading")

A tag can be associated with an object in the following ways:

*   Someone manually set the tag on the object using a CREATE <object> or ALTER <object> command. See [Set a tag](work.html#label-object-tagging-set).
    
*   The object inherited the tag from an object higher up in the Snowflake securable object hierarchy. For example, a warehouse in an account inherits tags set on the account. See [Tag inheritance](inheritance).
    
*   The tag was automatically propagated from one object to another. Tags can be propagated when an object depends on another object (for example, a view based on a tagged table) or when data moves from a tagged object to another object (for example, using a CTAS statement to create a table). See [Automatic tag propagation with user-defined tags](propagation).
    
*   The tag was automatically set on a column that was classified as containing sensitive data. To learn how sensitive data classification uses a tag map to set these tags, see [About classification tags](../classify-intro.html#label-classify-classification-tags).
    
*   Someone used the CREATE TABLE … LIKE or CREATE TABLE … CLONE command to create a table from an existing table with tags.

# Tags

**Feature Availability**

Self-Hosted DataHub

DataHub Cloud

Tags are informal, loosely controlled labels that help in search & discovery. They can be added to datasets, dataset schemas, or containers, for an easy way to label or categorize entities – without having to associate them to a broader business glossary or vocabulary.

Tags can help help you in:

*   Querying: Tagging a dataset with a phrase that a co-worker can use to query the same dataset
*   Mapping assets to a category or group of your choice

## Tags Setup, Prerequisites, and Permissions[​](#tags-setup-prerequisites-and-permissions "Direct link to Tags Setup, Prerequisites, and Permissions")

What you need to add tags:

*   **Edit Tags** metadata privilege to add tags at the entity level
*   **Edit Dataset Column Tags** to edit tags at the column level

You can create these privileges by creating a new [Metadata Policy](/docs/authorization/policies).

## Using DataHub Tags[​](#using-datahub-tags "Direct link to Using DataHub Tags")

### Adding a Tag[​](#adding-a-tag "Direct link to Adding a Tag")

To add a tag at the dataset or container level, simply navigate to the page for that entity and click on the **Add Tag** button.

![](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/datahub-project/static-assets/master/imgs/add-tag.png)

Type in the name of the tag you want to add. You can add a new tag, or add a tag that already exists (the autocomplete will pull up the tag if it already exists).

![](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/datahub-project/static-assets/master/imgs/add-tag-search.png)

Click on the "Add" button and you'll see the tag has been added!

![](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/datahub-project/static-assets/master/imgs/added-tag.png)

If you would like to add a tag at the schema level, hover over the "Tags" column for a schema until the "Add Tag" button shows up, and then follow the same flow as above.

![](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/datahub-project/static-assets/master/imgs/add-schema-tag.png)

### Removing a Tag[​](#removing-a-tag "Direct link to Removing a Tag")

To remove a tag, simply click on the "X" button in the tag. Then click "Yes" when prompted to confirm tag removal.

### Searching by a Tag[​](#searching-by-a-tag "Direct link to Searching by a Tag")

You can search for a tag in the search bar, and even filter entities by the presence of a specific tag.

![](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/datahub-project/static-assets/master/imgs/search-tag.png)

> AGENT

Good research done. Here's the honest answer:

## What Enterprise Data Systems Actually Label

**The labeling exists, but it's at different granularities — and none solve your exact problem.**

| System | What it labels | Granularity | Row-level? |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Snowflake** | Tags (key-value pairs) | Database → schema → table → column. Inherited downward. | No — tags are on objects, not individual rows |
| **BigQuery** | Labels (key-value pairs) | Dataset → table → view level only | No — no column or row level |
| **AWS Glue** | Data Catalog (auto-crawled metadata) | Database → table → column. Crawlers infer schemas. | No |
| **DataHub / OpenMetadata** | Tags + Business Glossary terms | Dataset → schema → column. Also supports "domains" for grouping. | No |
| **dbt** | Tags + meta + descriptions | Model → column level | No |

**The key gap you identified is real:** none of these systems label at the **row level**. They tell you "this table is owned by Marketing" or "this column contains PII" — but they can't tell you "rows 1–50,000 belong to Institution A and rows 50,001–100,000 belong to Institution B."

Your example of a single table containing data for two different institutions — that's a **multi-tenant data pattern**, and the "label" for that is literally just a column value like `institution_id = 'harvard'` or `department = 'engineering'`. No data governance platform treats column values as labels because that's just... querying.

## What This Means for Raven's Classification

There are actually **two distinct classification problems**:

**Problem 1: "Which tables/sources should feed which knowledge base?"**
This is the structural mapping — "the `support_tickets` table goes to the Support KB, the `product_docs` table goes to the Product KB." This is where Snowflake tags, dbt metadata, DataHub glossary terms ARE useful. If the enterprise already tagged their data, Raven reads those tags and auto-routes.

**Problem 2: "Within a single table, which rows go where?"**
This is your multi-tenant table scenario. No external labeling system solves this — it's a **filter condition**. The admin needs to tell Raven: "From the `documents` table, rows where `org = 'harvard'` go to Harvard's KB, rows where `org = 'mit'` go to MIT's KB."

## How Raven Should Handle This

Don't build a labeling system. Build a **connector configuration** with filter rules:

```yaml
connector: client-postgres
source: airbyte/postgres
tables:
  - name: support_tickets
    knowledge_base: "Support KB"          # whole table → one KB
    text_columns: [subject, body, resolution]
    
  - name: documents
    text_columns: [title, content]
    routing:                               # row-level routing
      column: institution
      rules:
        - value: "harvard"
          knowledge_base: "Harvard KB"
        - value: "mit"  
          knowledge_base: "MIT KB"
        - default: "Unclassified KB"
    
  - name: product_catalog
    knowledge_base: auto                   # LLM classifies on ingest
    text_columns: [name, description, specs]
```

Three routing modes:
1. **Static** — whole table → one KB (most common)
2. **Column-based** — route by a discriminator column value (your multi-tenant case)
3. **Auto** — LLM classifies each row during ingestion (expensive, use sparingly)

And when external catalogs exist (Snowflake tags, dbt, DataHub), Raven can **pre-populate** this config by reading the tags — the admin just confirms rather than building from scratch.

This is simpler than building a labeling system, covers your multi-tenant case, and leverages existing enterprise metadata where available. Does this match what you were envisioning?

> DEVELOPER

I don't want to build a labeling system on my own. We will just rely on something that is already there. If, by any chance, there are no labels that exist in a database like Postgres or something, then we can just give us a very simple labeling feature, or not even labeling. Basically, they will come up with the sections that they want to expose to their voice agent and their chatbot. Let's say it's a particular program in a college, so only MBA data needs to be enabled for voice and stuff, so then they can label it based on a column field for that institute or for that particular course or something like that, very simple.

Otherwise, we will always use Snowflake tags, dbt data hub, and what they already provide, and then the admins just have to select it and map it to a particular topic that they want to expose.

> AGENT

Exactly — keep it dead simple. Let me make sure I've got this right:

1. **If the enterprise has existing metadata** (Snowflake tags, dbt, DataHub, Glue Catalog) → Raven reads it, presents it to the admin as a selectable list, admin maps tags to knowledge bases. Zero labeling effort.

2. **If it's a raw database with no metadata** (plain Postgres, MySQL, WordPress) → Admin picks which tables/columns to expose, optionally filters by a column value (e.g., `course = 'MBA'`), maps to a knowledge base. That's it — no labeling system, just a filter + mapping UI.

Raven doesn't label data. Raven consumes labels that already exist, or lets the admin point at what they want exposed. The complexity lives in the source systems where it belongs.

One clarifying question before we wrap this topic: For the raw database case, do you envision the admin doing this mapping through the **Raven admin dashboard UI** (drag-drop table/column picker, dropdown for filter values), or is a **YAML/JSON config file** acceptable for the enterprise on-prem deployment? Or both — UI for cloud tier, config file for on-prem?

> DEVELOPER

I think Both would be great. I mean, if they are not tech-savvy, then I don't think they will go for YAML and stuff. If it is a self-hosting situation, then yeah, if they can self-host, I'm sure they can also configure it. You're correct that we will keep the labeling UI as part of the cloud-providing feature, and it will be paid. That is the next topic that I want to discuss with you: how are we going to separate some particular features only for paid enterprise users or pro users? How do we price, maybe according to the number of users that are there in the Keycloak? I don't think users will come to Keycloak, because strangers will be using the users on Keycloak; the users in Keycloak will only be the admin and the employees and stuff, so I don't know how we will monetize that.

> AGENT

Good pivot — this is a core product strategy question. Let me check what's already been researched on monetization.

| **Cons** | Revenue is unpredictable; customers have bill anxiety; hard to forecast; operationally complex (metering). |

| **Examples** | Vapi ($0.05/min), Retell ($0.07/min), Cohere (per-token) |

### Option A: Pure SaaS Subscription

|--------|--------|

## Appendix C: Key Decisions Summary

| Decision | Recommendation | Rationale |
|----------|---------------|-----------|
| **Primary revenue model** | Hybrid (subscription + usage overage) | Industry standard; predictable base + upside from power users |
| **Distribution model** | Open-core (free self-hosted + paid cloud) | Maximizes adoption; proven by Dify/GitLab/PostHog |
| **Starting infrastructure** | Hetzner (not AWS) | 5-10x cheaper; break-even with 2 customers vs. 5-6 |

### Raven Platform -- Monetization Strategy > 4. Revenue Model Options > Option C: Hybrid (Base Subscription + Usage Overage) -- RECOMMENDED
### Option C: Hybrid (Base Subscription + Usage Overage) -- RECOMMENDED

| Aspect | Detail |
|--------|--------|
| **How it works** | Fixed monthly subscription includes generous usage allowance. Overage charged per unit above the allowance. |
| **Pros** | Predictable base revenue; captures value from power users; generous allowance reduces bill anxiety; aligns with how most SaaS prices. |
| **Cons** | Slightly more complex billing; need usage metering infrastructure. |
| **Best for** | Platforms with variable usage patterns and both light/heavy users. |
| **Examples** | Chatbase (tiers + overage), PostHog (free allowance + pay-as-you-go), Vercel (hobby free + usage) |

You already have thorough monetization research. The key pieces relevant to your question:

**Your existing model (C + D Hybrid) already answers most of this:**

## Keycloak Users ≠ Your Billing Metric

You're right — Keycloak users are the **client's employees** (admins, support staff), not end-users. The strangers asking the chatbot questions are anonymous — they never touch Keycloak. So "per-seat" pricing based on Keycloak users doesn't work because:

- A college with 5 admin staff but 50,000 students querying the chatbot would pay for 5 seats — that's pennies
- A consultancy with 100 employees but only 200 chatbot queries/month would overpay

**Your existing pricing doc already got this right** — the value metrics are:

| Metric | Why it works |
|---|---|
| **Messages** (chatbot + voice queries) | Directly measures how much value end-users extract |
| **Documents** ingested | Measures how much knowledge the client is storing |
| **Knowledge bases** | Measures organizational complexity |
| **Voice minutes** | Directly maps to STT/TTS cost |
| **Storage** | Scales with data volume |

Keycloak seat count is useful only as a **secondary limit** on the admin dashboard (e.g., Pro = 3 dashboard users, Business = 10, Enterprise = unlimited) — not as the primary billing axis.

The connector system is a natural enterprise upsell — it's the feature that makes "bring Raven to your premise" worth paying $299+/month for. Self-hosters can configure YAML manually for free, but the UI, catalog integrations, and managed Airbyte infrastructure are paid.

These are smart monetization angles — you're essentially saying the **intelligence layer** (who's using the chatbot, what they're doing, lead conversion signals) is the paid value, not the chatbot itself. Let me structure what you just described:

## New Paid Feature Categories

**1. Lead Intelligence (Business/Enterprise)**
- PostHog/analytics captures anonymous visitor → chatbot interaction → intent signals
- Free tier: you see aggregate stats (total queries, popular topics)
- Paid tier: you get **individual lead profiles** — contact info if captured, conversation history, intent classification, engagement score
- Enterprise: export lead data to their CRM (Salesforce, HubSpot) via webhook

**2. Event Webhooks (Business/Enterprise)**
- `lead.generated` — fires when a stranger shows purchase intent or leaves contact info
- `lead.qualified` — fires when engagement crosses a scoring threshold
- `conversation.escalation` — fires when the bot can't answer and a human is needed
- Free: no webhooks. Paid: configurable webhook endpoints per event type.

**3. Stranger User Management (Business/Enterprise)**
- Block/ban specific users (by IP, fingerprint, or session ID)
- Rate limit per-user (not just global rate limiting)
- Flag suspicious behavior patterns
- Free: basic global rate limiting only. Paid: per-user controls + block lists.

**4. Advanced Security (Enterprise)**
- Custom WAF-style rules (geo-blocking, request pattern rules)
- DDoS protection beyond basic rate limiting (the eBPF XDP layer we discussed earlier actually fits here perfectly as an enterprise feature)
- IP allowlisting/denylisting
- Abuse detection and alerting
- Free: standard CORS + security headers + Valkey rate limiter. Paid: custom rules engine + advanced protection.

|---|---|---|---|---|

| **User management** | -- | -- | Block/ban users | + abuse detection |
| **Security** | Basic (CORS, rate limit) | Basic | + per-user rate limits | + custom rules, DDoS, WAF |

This is a genuinely differentiated pricing model — most RAG chatbot competitors (Chatbase, CustomGPT) don't offer lead intelligence or user-level controls at all. It positions Raven as a **revenue-generating tool** for the client, not just a cost center.

> AGENT

100% valid concern. The classic open-source business problem — your best users are the ones most capable of self-hosting and never paying you.

The industry has converged on a few models to prevent this. Let me give you the honest tradeoffs:

**Model A: License-based (GitLab model)**
- Open-source core is free for everyone
- Enterprise features (SSO, audit logs, connectors UI, lead intelligence) require a paid license key, even when self-hosted
- License key checked at runtime — features are in the code but gated
- GitLab CE is free, GitLab EE is $29/user/month even self-hosted

**Model B: Revenue threshold (Sentry/AG Grid model)**
- Free self-hosting under a revenue/employee threshold (e.g., < $10M ARR or < 50 employees)
- Above threshold, must purchase a license
- Enforced by honor + legal terms, not technical gates
- BSL (Business Source License) makes this legally enforceable

**Model C: Feature split (PostHog/Dify model)**
- Core is truly open source (MIT/Apache)
- Premium features live in a separate `ee/` directory with a proprietary license
- Self-hosters get the core. Enterprise features require a license regardless of where you deploy.

**For Raven, I'd recommend Model C with a revenue gate from Model B.** Here's why:

| What's free to self-host (always) | What requires a license (even self-hosted) |
|---|---|
| Full RAG pipeline | Airbyte connector UI + managed connectors |
| Chatbot widget | Lead intelligence (individual profiles, scoring) |
| Hybrid search | Event webhooks (lead.generated, etc.) |
| Voice agent | Advanced security rules (DDoS, WAF, per-user blocking) |
| BYOK LLM | Data catalog integration (Snowflake tags, dbt, DataHub) |
| Basic analytics (aggregate) | CRM export / third-party integrations |
| Manual file upload + URL scraping | ClickHouse + QBit vector scaling |
| Basic rate limiting + security | SSO (SAML/OIDC beyond Keycloak basic) |
| YAML connector config (DIY) | Audit logs + compliance reports |
| Single-org deployment | Multi-org / white-label |

The implementation is straightforward — a license check middleware in the Go service:

```
// Conceptual — not writing code, just illustrating the gate
if feature == "lead_intelligence" && !license.Valid() {
    return ErrRequiresLicense
}
```

Enterprise self-hosted pricing could be:

| | Self-hosted Free | Self-hosted Pro | Self-hosted Enterprise |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Who** | Startups, students, < $5M ARR | Growing companies | Enterprises, > $5M ARR |
| **Price** | $0 | $99/month | $499+/month |
| **Includes** | Core features | + lead intelligence, webhooks, connectors | + SSO, audit, SLA, security rules, priority support |
| **Upstream updates** | Community releases | Stable releases + patch support | Priority patches + dedicated support channel |
| **Legal** | Apache 2.0 core | License key | License key + SLA agreement |

The revenue threshold ($5M ARR) is enforced via license terms, not code. A startup below the threshold uses Pro features free as part of a "startup program" — this builds goodwill and converts them to paying when they grow. Above the threshold, legal terms require a license. This is how Sentry's BSL and AG Grid's license work — it's standard and enforceable.

This way a smart SaaS company can self-host the core forever, but the moment they want lead intelligence, webhooks, connectors, or advanced security — the features that actually generate revenue for them — they're paying you.

Now, your enforcement question is the critical one. Let me answer it honestly.

## You Cannot Technically Prevent It. That's Not How Open-Source Companies Do It.

Every open-source company — GitLab, Sentry, PostHog, Dify — faces this exact problem. Here's how they all solve it:

**The code is split into two directories with two different licenses:**

```
raven/
├── internal/           ← Apache 2.0 (truly open source)
├── cmd/
├── pkg/
├── ee/                 ← Raven Enterprise License (source-available, NOT open source)
│   ├── LICENSE          → "You may view this code. You may not use it
│   │                       in production without a valid license key."
│   ├── lead/            → lead intelligence
│   ├── webhooks/        → event webhooks
│   ├── connectors/      → connector UI + catalog integration
│   ├── security/        → advanced WAF rules, DDoS
│   ├── audit/           → audit logs
│   └── sso/             → SAML/OIDC enterprise SSO
```

The `ee/` directory is **visible on GitHub** (source-available) but the license says: you can read it, contribute to it, even run it in development — but production use requires a paid license.

**Can a smart company clone the repo, rip out the license check, and run `ee/` features for free?**

Yes, technically. But:

1. **They're in legal violation.** The `ee/` directory has a proprietary license, not Apache 2.0. This is legally enforceable — GitLab, Elastic, and MongoDB have all sent cease-and-desist letters successfully.

2. **Every upstream update becomes a merge conflict nightmare.** You'll keep evolving `ee/` features. They'd have to maintain their fork forever, manually resolving conflicts on every release. The engineering cost of this exceeds the license cost within months.

3. **Large companies won't risk it.** The companies worth worrying about ($5M+ ARR) have legal departments. Legal departments don't approve running software in license violation — the liability exposure isn't worth saving $499/month.

4. **The people who would strip flags were never going to pay anyway.** A scrappy 2-person startup stripping your license check is not lost revenue — they'd have just used an alternative. The enterprise deal you'd actually lose is the one where procurement checks the license and says "this is fine."

## How the License Check Actually Works

It's not a feature flag in the traditional sense. It's a **signed license key** that your server validates:

```
Client buys license → You generate signed JWT with:
  - org_id
  - tier (pro/enterprise)  
  - expiry date
  - feature list
  - HMAC signature (only you can generate)

Raven binary on startup:
  - Reads license key from env/config
  - Validates signature (can't be forged without your private key)
  - Unlocks ee/ features accordingly
  - No license or invalid? → ee/ features return 403
```

Can they patch the binary to skip the check? Yes. But that requires:
- Forking the repo
- Modifying the license validation code
- Rebuilding from source
- Maintaining that fork forever
- Accepting legal liability

99% of companies will just pay. The 1% who won't were never your customers.

## Real-World Precedent

| Company | How they split | Does it work? |
|---|---|---|
| **GitLab** | `ee/` directory, EE license | Yes — $500M+ ARR |
| **PostHog** | `ee/` directory, PostHog Enterprise License | Yes — $100M+ valuation |
| **Sentry** | BSL (Business Source License) — converts to open source after 3 years | Yes — $100M+ ARR |
| **Dify** | Enterprise features gated by license | Yes — fastest growing AI framework |

This is the proven model. It's not technically unbreakable — it's **legally and economically** unbreakable for the companies that matter.

200	| **Examples** | Dify ($59/$159), Flowise ($35), AnythingLLM ($6.99/seat) |
201	
202	### Option B: Pure Usage-Based (Pay Per Use)
203	
204	| Aspect | Detail |
205	|--------|--------|
206	| **How it works** | No subscription. Charge per message, per document ingested, per voice minute. |
207	| **Pros** | Aligns cost with value; low barrier to entry; scales naturally. |
208	| **Cons** | Revenue is unpredictable; customers have bill anxiety; hard to forecast; operationally complex (metering). |
209	| **Best for** | Commodity services where per-unit economics are clear. |
210	| **Examples** | Vapi ($0.05/min), Retell ($0.07/min), Cohere (per-token) |
211	
212	### Option C: Hybrid (Base Subscription + Usage Overage) -- RECOMMENDED
213	
214	| Aspect | Detail |
215	|--------|--------|
216	| **How it works** | Fixed monthly subscription includes generous usage allowance. Overage charged per unit above the allowance. |
217	| **Pros** | Predictable base revenue; captures value from power users; generous allowance reduces bill anxiety; aligns with how most SaaS prices. |
218	| **Cons** | Slightly more complex billing; need usage metering infrastructure. |
219	| **Best for** | Platforms with variable usage patterns and both light/heavy users. |
220	| **Examples** | Chatbase (tiers + overage), PostHog (free allowance + pay-as-you-go), Vercel (hobby free + usage) |
221	
222	### Option D: Open-Core (Free Self-Hosted + Paid Cloud Features)
223	
224	| Aspect | Detail |
225	|--------|--------|
226	| **How it works** | Core platform is open-source and free to self-host. Cloud-managed version adds convenience features. Certain "enterprise" features only available in paid edition. |
227	| **Pros** | Massive developer adoption; community contributions; trust through transparency; viral distribution. |
228	| **Cons** | Revenue conversion is low (typically 1-5% of users pay); must carefully choose what's free vs. paid; support burden from free users. |
229	| **Best for** | Developer tools where adoption is the primary moat. |
230	| **Examples** | Dify (free self-hosted, paid cloud), GitLab (CE vs EE), PostHog (open-core + cloud) |
231	
232	### Option E: Marketplace / Premium Add-Ons
233	
234	| Aspect | Detail |
235	|--------|--------|
236	| **How it works** | Base platform is subscription-priced. Premium integrations, templates, or connectors are sold separately or as add-ons. |
237	| **Pros** | Additional revenue stream; partners can build and sell; extensible ecosystem. |
238	| **Cons** | Requires ecosystem scale to be meaningful; complex to manage; early-stage distraction. |
239	| **Best for** | Mature platforms with established user base. |
240	| **Examples** | Shopify App Store, WordPress plugins, Slack Marketplace |
241	
242	### Recommended Model: C + D Hybrid
243	
244	**Combine Open-Core (D) with Hybrid Subscription + Usage (C):**
245	
246	1. **Self-hosted:** Free and open-source (all features, no artificial limits)
247	2. **Cloud-managed:** Hybrid subscription with usage allowances and overage
248	3. **Enterprise self-hosted:** Paid license for enterprise features (SSO, audit logs, SLA)
249

425	## 6. Break-Even Calculations
426	
427	### Scenario A: 10 Customers on Hetzner CCX33 ($55/month)
428	
429	**Fixed costs:**
430	| Item | Monthly Cost |
431	|------|-------------|
432	| Hetzner CCX33 | $55 |
433	| Domain (amortized) | $2 |
434	| AWS SES (email) | $1 |
435	| **Total** | **$58/month** |
436	
437	**Revenue scenarios (10 customers):**
438	
439	| Customer Mix | Monthly Revenue | Stripe Fees (2.9%+$0.30) | Net Revenue | Profit/Loss |
440	|-------------|----------------|--------------------------|-------------|-------------|
441	| 10x Pro ($29) | $290 | $11.41 | $278.59 | **+$220.59** |
442	| 5x Pro + 5x Free | $145 | $5.71 | $139.29 | **+$81.29** |
443	| 3x Business + 7x Free | $297 | $9.57 | $287.43 | **+$229.43** |
444	| 2x Pro + 1x Business | $157 | $5.57 | $151.43 | **+$93.43** |
445	| 10x Free | $0 | $0 | $0 | **-$58** |
446	
447	**Break-even point:** 2 Pro customers ($58 revenue) covers costs. With 10 paying customers at any paid tier, Raven is profitable from day one on Hetzner.
448	
449	**Minimum price per customer for break-even with 10 customers:**
450	- $58 / 10 = $5.80/customer/month (before Stripe fees)
451	- With Stripe: $58 / (10 * (1 - 0.029)) - $0.30 = ~$6.27/customer/month
452	
453	This means even a hypothetical $7/month "Starter" tier would break even at 10 customers. The proposed $29 Pro tier provides **5x headroom** above break-even.
454	
455	### Scenario B: 10 Customers on Hetzner CPX41 ($28/month, Budget Option)
456	
457	| Item | Monthly Cost |
458	|------|-------------|
459	| Hetzner CPX41 | $28 |
460	| Domain + Email | $3 |
461	| **Total** | **$31/month** |
462	
463	**Break-even:** Just 2 Pro customers ($58 revenue - $2.38 Stripe = $55.62 net). Even 1 Business customer ($99 - $3.17 = $95.83 net) covers costs 3x over.
464	
465	### Scenario C: Scaling to 100 Customers
466	
467	**Assumption:** Upgrade to Hetzner AX42 dedicated ($50/month) + backup VPS ($28/month) = $78/month.

584	
585	## 7. Growth Strategy -- First 10 Paying Customers
586	
587	### Phase 0: Pre-Launch (Months 1-2)
588	
589	**Goal:** Build in public, create awareness, validate demand.
590	
591	#### 7.1 Open-Source Community Launch
592	
593	1. **GitHub repository with excellent README** -- Include animated GIF of the chatbot widget, one-command Docker Compose setup, architecture diagram. Target 100 stars in the first week.
594	
595	2. **"Show HN" on Hacker News** -- Title: "Raven -- Open-source multi-tenant RAG platform with embeddable chatbot (Go + Python, self-hostable)" -- The self-hostable + BYOK angle resonates strongly on HN.
596	
597	3. **Product Hunt launch** -- Prepare assets, screenshots, demo video. Target top 5 of the day. Schedule for Tuesday/Wednesday (highest engagement days).
598	
599	4. **r/selfhosted, r/LocalLLaMA, r/ChatGPT, r/artificial** -- Reddit is where self-hosted AI tool enthusiasts congregate. Cross-post the GitHub repo with a "I built this" narrative.
600	
601	5. **Dev.to / Hashnode / Medium technical articles:**
602	   - "Building a Multi-Tenant RAG Platform with Go and pgvector"
603	   - "Why Hybrid Search (BM25 + Vector) Beats Pure Vector Search"
604	   - "Self-Hosted AI Chatbot: From PDF Upload to Embedded Widget in 5 Minutes"
605	
606	6. **Discord / Slack community** -- Create a Raven community server. Active communities around open-source tools are the highest-converting free channels.
607	
608	#### 7.2 Content Marketing (The Knowledge-Base Irony)
609	
610	Raven is a knowledge-base platform. **Use Raven to power Raven's own documentation chatbot.** This is simultaneously:
611	- A product demo (visitors see the chatbot working live)
612	- Content marketing (the docs are searchable and findable)
613	- Social proof ("eat your own dogfood")
614	
615	Embed the `<raven-chat>` widget on the Raven documentation site. Every visitor interaction is a demo.
616	
617	#### 7.3 Direct Outreach to Early Adopters
618	
619	**Target segments for first 10 customers:**
620	
621	| Segment | Why They Need Raven | Where to Find Them | Willingness to Pay |
622	|---------|--------------------|--------------------|-------------------|
623	| **SaaS companies with documentation** | Want AI chatbot for docs without building it | ProductHunt, Indie Hackers, MicroConf | High -- it is a support cost reducer |
624	| **Digital agencies** | Build chatbots for clients; need white-label multi-tenant | Agency directories, Clutch.co, Upwork | High -- they resell to clients |
625	| **E-commerce stores** | Product FAQ chatbot; reduce support tickets | Shopify app store (future), e-commerce forums | Medium -- price-sensitive |
626	| **Developer tool companies** | Technical docs chatbot (Mendable/Inkeep alternative) | GitHub, Dev.to, HN | High -- they already pay for Mendable |
627	| **Consultants / freelancers** | Offer AI chatbot as a service to clients | Freelance communities, LinkedIn | Medium -- value white-labeling |
628	| **Internal knowledge management** | Company wiki chatbot for employees | IT departments, Knowledge Management communities | High -- enterprise budgets |
629

708	## 8. Implementation Roadmap
709	
710	### Billing Implementation Priority
711	
712	| Priority | Feature | Tool | Effort |
713	|----------|---------|------|--------|
714	| **P0 (MVP)** | Stripe Checkout for subscriptions | Stripe Billing API | 2-3 days |
715	| **P0 (MVP)** | Webhook handler for payment events | Stripe Webhooks | 1-2 days |
716	| **P0 (MVP)** | Org <-> Stripe Customer mapping | Go API + PostgreSQL | 1 day |
717	| **P0 (MVP)** | Plan/tier enforcement (feature gates) | Go middleware | 2-3 days |
718	| **P1 (v1.0)** | Usage metering (message count, document count) | Go API counters -> PostgreSQL | 2-3 days |
719	| **P1 (v1.0)** | Usage-based overage billing | Stripe Usage Records API | 2-3 days |
720	| **P1 (v1.0)** | Self-service plan management UI | Vue.js + Stripe Customer Portal | 1-2 days |
721	| **P2 (v1.1)** | Annual billing toggle | Stripe price switching | 1 day |
722	| **P2 (v1.1)** | Voice minute metering (Phase 2) | Go API + LiveKit hooks | 2 days |
723	| **P3 (future)** | Lago/OpenMeter for advanced metering | Lago API | 1-2 weeks |
724	| **P3 (future)** | Enterprise invoice billing (net-30) | Stripe Invoicing | 1-2 days |
725	
726	### Usage Metering Architecture
727	
728	```
729	[Chatbot Query] --> [Go API] --> increment counter --> [PostgreSQL: usage_records]
730	                                                         |
731	[Document Upload] --> [Go API] --> increment counter ----+
732	                                                         |
733	[Voice Session] --> [LiveKit webhook] --> log minutes ---+
734	                                                         |
735	                    [Cron: hourly] --> report to Stripe Usage Records API
736	                                         |
737	                    [Cron: daily] --> check limits --> soft-cap warnings (email)
738	                                         |
739	                    [Cron: monthly] --> Stripe invoice with overages
740	```
741	
742	### Feature Gate Implementation
743	
744	Feature gates should be enforced at the Go API middleware layer:
745	
746	```
747	Request -> Auth middleware -> Plan middleware -> Handler
748	                               |
749	                               +-- Check org's plan tier

808	## Appendix C: Key Decisions Summary
809	
810	| Decision | Recommendation | Rationale |
811	|----------|---------------|-----------|
812	| **Primary revenue model** | Hybrid (subscription + usage overage) | Industry standard; predictable base + upside from power users |
813	| **Distribution model** | Open-core (free self-hosted + paid cloud) | Maximizes adoption; proven by Dify/GitLab/PostHog |
814	| **Starting infrastructure** | Hetzner (not AWS) | 5-10x cheaper; break-even with 2 customers vs. 5-6 |
815	| **Entry price point** | $29/month | Above Chatbase ($19) to signal quality; below CustomGPT ($89) for accessibility |
816	| **Free tier generosity** | 500 messages, 50 docs, 1 KB | Generous enough for testing; forces upgrade for any real usage |
817	| **Voice pricing (Phase 2)** | Included minutes + per-minute overage | Matches Vapi/Retell model but with included minutes to reduce friction |
818	| **First customers** | SaaS docs teams + digital agencies | Highest willingness to pay; clear pain point; referenceable logos |
819	| **Enterprise self-hosted** | $499/month | Captures enterprise value without cloud hosting costs |
820	
821	---
822	
823	*This document should be revisited after the first 10 paying customers to validate assumptions and adjust pricing based on actual usage patterns and customer feedback.*

### Raven Platform -- Implementation Plan > Milestone 1: Project Scaffolding (Week 1-2) > Task 1.2: Initialize Python AI Worker with gRPC Server Skeleton
#### Task 1.2: Initialize Python AI Worker with gRPC Server Skeleton

- **Milestone:** Project Scaffolding
- **Priority:** P0 (blocker)
- **Estimate:** 2 days
- **Dependencies:** None
- **Description:** Create the `ai-worker/` directory with a Python 3.12 project structure:
  ```
  ai-worker/
    raven_worker/
      __init__.py
      server.py         -- gRPC server entry point
      services/
        __init__.py
        embedding.py    -- embedding service stub
        rag.py          -- RAG query service stub
      processors/

### Raven Platform -- Implementation Plan > Milestone 1: Project Scaffolding (Week 1-2) > Task 1.1: Initialize Go Module with Gin and Project Structure
#### Task 1.1: Initialize Go Module with Gin and Project Structure

- **Milestone:** Project Scaffolding
- **Priority:** P0 (blocker)
- **Estimate:** 2 days
- **Dependencies:** None
- **Description:** Initialize the Go module (`github.com/raven-platform/raven`) with Gin web framework. Set up the canonical Go project layout:
  ```
  cmd/
    api/          -- main.go entry point for the Go API server
  internal/
    config/       -- viper-based configuration loading
    handler/      -- Gin HTTP handlers (organized by domain)
    middleware/    -- JWT, RBAC, rate limiting, tenant resolution
    model/        -- domain types and database models
    repository/   -- database access layer (pgx + sqlc generated)
    service/      -- business logic layer
    grpc/         -- gRPC client for Python AI worker
  pkg/
    apierror/     -- shared error types and HTTP error responses
    validator/    -- input validation helpers
  migrations/     -- goose SQL migration files
  proto/          -- protobuf definitions (shared with Python worker)
  ```
  Install core dependencies: Gin, pgx v5, go-redis v9, viper, goose v3, grpc-go. Configure `air` for hot-reload during development. Create a health check endpoint (`GET /healthz`) that returns 200.
- **Acceptance criteria:**
  - `go build ./cmd/api` produces a working binary
  - `air` hot-reload works in development
  - `GET /healthz` returns `{"status": "ok"}`
  - All core dependencies are in `go.mod`
  - Project structure matches the layout above
- **Labels:** `backend`

All done. Here is a summary of what was completed:

**Files created (11 total):**

- `/LICENSE` -- Root Apache 2.0 license (did not exist previously)
- `/ee/LICENSE` -- Raven Enterprise License (source-available, production use requires license key)
- `/ee/README.md` -- Documents enterprise features, tiers, and licensing model
- `/ee/lead/lead.go` -- Lead intelligence package stub
- `/ee/webhooks/webhooks.go` -- Event webhooks package stub
- `/ee/connectors/connectors.go` -- Connector UI + data catalog package stub
- `/ee/security/security.go` -- Advanced WAF/DDoS package stub
- `/ee/audit/audit.go` -- Audit logs + compliance reports package stub
- `/ee/sso/sso.go` -- Enterprise SSO (SAML/OIDC) package stub
- `/ee/analytics/analytics.go` -- Advanced analytics package stub
- `/ee/licensing/license.go` -- License validation with `Tier`, `License` struct, `Valid()`, and `HasFeature()` methods

**Note on directory naming:** The license validation package directory is `ee/licensing/` (not `ee/license/`) because macOS uses a case-insensitive filesystem and `ee/LICENSE` (the license text file) and `ee/license/` (the directory) would collide. 1	// Package license provides license key validation for Raven Enterprise features.
2	// License keys are signed JWTs containing org_id, tier, expiry, and feature list.
3	//
4	// This package is part of Raven Enterprise Edition (Go backend).
5	package license
6	
7	// Tier represents the enterprise license tier.
8	type Tier string
9	
10	const (
11		// TierBusiness enables Business-tier enterprise features.
12		TierBusiness Tier = "business"
13		// TierEnterprise enables all enterprise features.
14		TierEnterprise Tier = "enterprise"
15	)
16	
17	// License represents a validated Raven enterprise license.
18	type License struct {
19		OrgID     string   `json:"org_id"`
20		Tier      Tier     `json:"tier"`
21		Features  []string `json:"features"`
22		ExpiresAt int64    `json:"expires_at"`
23	}
24	
25	// Valid reports whether the license is present and not expired.
26	func (l *License) Valid() bool {
27		return l != nil && l.OrgID != "" && l.ExpiresAt > 0
28	}
29	
30	// HasFeature reports whether the license grants access to the named feature.
31	func (l *License) HasFeature(feature string) bool {
32		if l == nil {
33			return false
34		}
35		if l.Tier == TierEnterprise {
36			return true
37		}
38		for _, f := range l.Features {
39			if f == feature {
40				return true
41			}
42		}
43		return false
44	}
45	
```

  "content": "// Package license provides license key validation for Raven Enterprise features.\n// License keys are signed JWTs containing org_id, tier, expiry, and feature list.\n//\n// This package is part of Raven Enterprise Edition (Go backend).\npackage license\n\nimport \"time\"\n\n// Tier represents the enterprise license tier.\ntype Tier string\n\nconst (\n\t// TierBusiness enables Business-tier enterprise features.\n\tTierBusiness Tier = \"business\"\n\t// TierEnterprise enables all enterprise features.\n\tTierEnterprise Tier = \"enterprise\"\n)\n\n// License represents a validated Raven enterprise license.\ntype License struct {\n\tOrgID     string   `json:\"org_id\"`\n\tTier      Tier     `json:\"tier\"`\n\tFeatures  []string `json:\"features\"`\n\tExpiresAt int64    `json:\"expires_at\"`\n}\n\n// Valid reports whether the license is present and not expired.\nfunc (l *License) Valid() bool {\n\treturn l != nil && l.OrgID != \"\" && l.ExpiresAt > time.Now().Unix()\n}\n\n// HasFeature reports whether the license grants access to the named feature.\nfunc (l *License) HasFeature(feature string) bool {\n\tif !l.Valid() {\n\t\treturn false\n\t}\n\tif l.Tier == TierEnterprise {\n\t\treturn true\n\t}\n\tfor _, f := range l.Features {\n\t\tif f == feature {\n\t\t\treturn true\n\t\t}\n\t}\n\treturn false\n}\n"
}

**Expired licenses are incorrectly validated.**

The `Valid()` method only checks whether `ExpiresAt > 0` but never compares it against the current time. This means a license with an expiration timestamp in the past will still return `true`, contradicting the documented behavior ("not expired").

<details>
<summary>🔧 Proposed fix</summary>

```diff
 package license
+
+import "time"
@@
 func (l *License) Valid() bool {
-	return l != nil && l.OrgID != "" && l.ExpiresAt > 0
+	if l == nil || l.OrgID == "" {
+		return false
+	}
+	return l.ExpiresAt > time.Now().Unix()
 }
```
</details>

1	// Package license provides license key validation for Raven Enterprise features.
2	// License keys are signed JWTs containing org_id, tier, expiry, and feature list.
3	//
4	// This package is part of Raven Enterprise Edition (Go backend).
5	package license
6	
7	import "time"
8	
9	// Tier represents the enterprise license tier.
10	type Tier string
11	
12	const (
13		// TierBusiness enables Business-tier enterprise features.
14		TierBusiness Tier = "business"
15		// TierEnterprise enables all enterprise features.
16		TierEnterprise Tier = "enterprise"
17	)
18	
19	// License represents a validated Raven enterprise license.
20	type License struct {
21		OrgID     string   `json:"org_id"`
22		Tier      Tier     `json:"tier"`
23		Features  []string `json:"features"`
24		ExpiresAt int64    `json:"expires_at"`
25	}
26	
27	// Valid reports whether the license is present and not expired.
28	func (l *License) Valid() bool {
29		return l != nil && l.OrgID != "" && l.ExpiresAt > time.Now().Unix()
30	}
31	
32	// HasFeature reports whether the license grants access to the named feature.
33	func (l *License) HasFeature(feature string) bool {
34		if !l.Valid() {
35			return false
36		}
37		if l.Tier == TierEnterprise {
38			return true
39		}
40		for _, f := range l.Features {
41			if f == feature {
42				return true
43			}
44		}
45		return false
46	}
47	
```

1	# Raven Enterprise Edition
2	
3	Raven uses an open-core model: the core platform is Apache 2.0 (free for everyone), while enterprise features live in `ee/` subdirectories under a separate license. Enterprise features require a valid license key for production use — see [ee-LICENSE](./ee-LICENSE) for full terms.

10	| Vue.js frontend | [`frontend/src/ee/`](./frontend/src/ee/README.md) | TypeScript/Vue | Lead UI, connector config, analytics dashboards, security management |

# Roadmap

## Phase Overview

| Phase | Scope | Timeline |
|-------|-------|----------|
| **Phase 1: MVP (Chatbot)** | Core API, ingestion pipeline, embeddable chatbot, admin dashboard, SaaS infra | 8-12 weeks |
| **Phase 2: Voice Agent** | LiveKit, STT/TTS, voice sessions, email notifications, smart caching | 4-6 weeks after Phase 1 |
| **v1.0 GA Readiness** | Error tracking, API docs, CDN, status page, virus scanning, secrets mgmt | Alongside Phase 2/3 |
| **Phase 3: WebRTC/WhatsApp** | WhatsApp Business Calling API, LiveKit bridging, browser WebRTC | 4-6 weeks after Phase 2 |
| **Phase 4: Knowledge Graph** | Neo4j, entity extraction, multi-hop queries | Future |
| **Phase 5: Cloud Managed** | AWS Terraform, hosted offering, pricing, i18n, a11y | Future |

## Phase 1 -- MVP (Chatbot)

**Core:** Organization + Workspace + Knowledge Base CRUD, Keycloak auth, PostgreSQL 18 + pgvector

**Ingestion:** File upload (PDF, DOCX, images), URL scraping (Crawl4AI), chunking, multi-provider embeddings (BYOK)

**Retrieval:** Hybrid search (pgvector + BM25 + RRF fusion), reranking

**Interaction:** Embeddable `<raven-chat>` web component with SSE streaming, API key auth

**Dashboard:** Vue.js + Tailwind Plus admin UI, chatbot configurator, test sandbox, analytics

**SaaS Infra:** Stripe billing, SSL/TLS, backups, legal pages, rate limiting, scheduled jobs

## Phase 2 -- Voice Agent + Smart Caching

**Voice:** LiveKit Agents, Deepgram/faster-whisper STT, Cartesia/Piper TTS, Silero VAD

**Email:** Conversation summaries via AWS SES, "resume conversation" links

**Smart Cache:** Semantic response cache (pgvector similarity) to minimize LLM API costs, in-database LLM adaptation for personalized cached responses

## Phase 3 -- WebRTC / WhatsApp

WhatsApp Business Calling API (WebRTC native), LiveKit room bridging, browser "call the assistant" button

## Milestones

See [GitHub Milestones](https://github.com/ravencloak-org/Raven/milestones) for detailed task tracking.
```
