235	| Criterion | PostHog | OpenObserve |
236	|-----------|---------|-------------|
237	| **Purpose** | Product analytics, feature flags, A/B tests | Logs, metrics, traces, RUM |
238	| **They solve different problems** | User behavior & product decisions | System observability & debugging |
239	| **License (core)** | MIT (permissive) | AGPL-3.0 (copyleft, network clause) |
240	| **Enterprise license** | Proprietary (ee/ dir) | Commercial (eliminates AGPL) |
241	| **SaaS-safe?** | Yes (MIT core) | Yes if unmodified; caution if modified |
242	| **Self-host memory** | 4 GB minimum (multi-container) | ~256-512 MB minimum (single binary) |
243	| **Edge-deployable?** | No (too heavy) | Yes (excellent fit) |
244	| **Cloud free tier** | 1M events, 5K replays, 1M flag requests/mo | 200 GB/day ingestion |
245	| **OpenTelemetry** | Not native (event-based model) | Native OTLP (built on OTel) |
246	| **Go SDK** | Yes | Yes (via OTel SDK + OTLP export) |
247	| **Python SDK** | Yes | Yes (OTel SDK + dedicated Python SDK) |
248	| **Docker deploy** | Yes (Docker Compose, multi-container) | Yes (single container) |
249	| **Latest version** | Rolling releases (`posthog-live-*`) | v0.70.1 stable, v0.80.0-rc1 |
250	| **GitHub stars** | ~32,200 | ~18,400 |
251	
252	### Recommendation
253	
254	These tools are **complementary, not competing**:
255	
256	1. **PostHog** for product analytics: Use **PostHog Cloud** (free tier is generous for early-stage). The self-hosted option is too heavy for edge deployment and lacks support guarantees. The MIT license makes it worry-free.
257	
258	2. **OpenObserve** for observability: **Self-host via Docker** for edge deployments where resource efficiency matters. Its single-binary Rust architecture, native OpenTelemetry support, and minimal memory footprint make it ideal for Raven's edge nodes. Use the AGPL open-source edition without modification to avoid license complications, or evaluate the Enterprise edition if AGPL is a policy concern.
259	
260	3. **Integration pattern:**
261	   - Raven Go services -> `go.opentelemetry.io/otel` SDK -> OpenObserve (OTLP) for traces/metrics/logs
262	   - Raven Python services -> `opentelemetry-sdk` -> OpenObserve (OTLP) for traces/metrics/logs
263	   - Raven frontend/product -> PostHog JS SDK -> PostHog Cloud for product analytics
264	   - Feature flags -> PostHog feature flags API -> Raven services
265	
266	---
267	
268	## License Risk Summary
269	
270	| Tool | License | Risk Level | Notes |
271	|------|---------|------------|-------|
272	| PostHog (core) | MIT | **None** | Fully permissive |
273	| PostHog (ee/) | Proprietary | **Low** | Only if using enterprise features without subscription |
274	| OpenObserve (OSS) | AGPL-3.0 | **Low-Medium** | Safe if used unmodified as infrastructure; modifications must be AGPL |
275	| OpenObserve (Enterprise) | Commercial | **None** | Standard commercial license |
276	
277	**Neither tool uses SSPL.** PostHog's MIT core is the cleanest license. OpenObserve's AGPL is manageable for infrastructure use but requires awareness of the modification disclosure requirement.
278

{
  "replace_all": false,
  "file_path": "/Users/jobinlawrance/Project/raven/docs/research/analytics-observability-research.md",
  "old_string": "### Recommendation\n\nThese tools are **complementary, not competing**:\n\n1. **PostHog** for product analytics: Use **PostHog Cloud** (free tier is generous for early-stage). The self-hosted option is too heavy for edge deployment and lacks support guarantees. The MIT license makes it worry-free.\n\n2. **OpenObserve** for observability: **Self-host via Docker** for edge deployments where resource efficiency matters. Its single-binary Rust architecture, native OpenTelemetry support, and minimal memory footprint make it ideal for Raven's edge nodes. Use the AGPL open-source edition without modification to avoid license complications, or evaluate the Enterprise edition if AGPL is a policy concern.\n\n3. **Integration pattern:**\n   - Raven Go services -> `go.opentelemetry.io/otel` SDK -> OpenObserve (OTLP) for traces/metrics/logs\n   - Raven Python services -> `opentelemetry-sdk` -> OpenObserve (OTLP) for traces/metrics/logs\n   - Raven frontend/product -> PostHog JS SDK -> PostHog Cloud for product analytics\n   - Feature flags -> PostHog feature flags API -> Raven services\n\n---\n\n## License Risk Summary",
  "new_string": "### Recommendation (Initial — superseded by two-tier analysis below)\n\nPostHog for product analytics (Cloud). OpenObserve for edge observability. SigNoz for cloud/enterprise observability.\n\n> See **Revised Recommendation (Two-Tier Deployment)** section below.\n\n---\n\n## 3. SigNoz — ClickHouse-native Observability (Cloud Tier Candidate)\n\n**Repository:** https://github.com/SigNoz/signoz\n**Storage:** ClickHouse (columnar OLAP)\n**License:** Apache 2.0 (core) + proprietary Enterprise edition\n\n### Architecture\n\nSigNoz OTel Collector (accepts Jaeger, Zipkin, OpenCensus, OTLP) → ClickHouse → SigNoz binary (API + React frontend + Alertmanager + Ruler)\n\n### Why ClickHouse Matters at Scale\n\n| Scenario | OpenObserve (Parquet/DataFusion) | SigNoz (ClickHouse) |\n|----------|----------------------------------|---------------------|\n| \"Last 5 min errors\" dashboard | Fast | Sub-second — data pre-indexed on write |\n| Aggregate across 1B+ spans | Good | Faster — vectorized columnar execution |\n| High-cardinality trace lookup | Fine | Better — sparse + skip indexes |\n| Storage cost (cloud) | Lower (Parquet on S3) | Higher (ClickHouse block storage) |\n| Memory floor | ~256 MB | ~2–4 GB (ClickHouse alone) |\n\n### Resource Requirements (Self-Host)\n\n- Minimum: 2 vCPU, 4 GB RAM (ClickHouse alone)\n- Recommended production: 4 vCPU, 8 GB RAM\n- Not viable on Raspberry Pi — cloud server only\n\n### Quickwit (Evaluated and Rejected)\n\nQuickwit is a Rust-based search engine using object storage (S3) with native Jaeger backend support. Architecturally similar to OpenObserve (not ClickHouse-based). **Acquired by Datadog in 2025** — project's independent roadmap is effectively dead. Not recommended for building a self-hosted product on top of.\n\n### SigNoz vs Uptrace (both ClickHouse-based)\n\nUptrace is another ClickHouse-native APM (AGPL, accepts Jaeger + OTLP). Leaner than SigNoz but much smaller community. SigNoz is the safer long-term bet.\n\n---\n\n## Revised Recommendation (Two-Tier Deployment)\n\nRaven has two deployment contexts with different constraints:\n\n### Cloud Tier (Enterprise Users) — No Memory Constraints\n\n**Observability: SigNoz** — ClickHouse gives sub-second dashboards at multi-tenant scale. All three signals. Accepts OTLP natively. Apache 2.0 core.\n\n```\nRaven Go services  → OTel SDK (OTLP) → SigNoz OTel Collector → ClickHouse\nRaven AI worker    → OTel SDK (OTLP) → SigNoz OTel Collector → ClickHouse\nRaven frontend     → PostHog JS SDK  → PostHog Cloud\n```\n\n### Edge / Self-Hostable Tier — User's Choice\n\n**Default: OpenObserve** (single binary, ~256–512 MB, OTLP native). Observability stack is opt-in and user-configurable. OTel endpoint is an env var — users can point at any OTLP-compatible backend.\n\n---\n\n## License Risk Summary"
}
```

{

  "file_path": "/Users/jobinlawrance/Project/raven/docs/wiki/Roadmap.md",
  "old_string": "## Milestones\n\nSee [GitHub Milestones](https://github.com/ravencloak-org/Raven/milestones) for detailed task tracking.",
  "new_string": "## Raven Pro: Enterprise Connectors (Future)\n\nOn-premise and hybrid deployment model with data connectors for enterprise clients.\n\n**Deployment Models:**\n- Cloud-with-agent: Raven cloud + secure agent on client network\n- On-premise: Full Raven stack inside client infrastructure\n\n**Data Connectors (Airbyte-powered):**\n- 700+ source connectors via embedded Airbyte (MIT core)\n- Databases: PostgreSQL, MySQL, MongoDB, ClickHouse, Oracle, MSSQL, Snowflake, BigQuery\n- SaaS: Salesforce, HubSpot, Confluence, Notion, Jira, Google Drive, SharePoint\n- Files: S3, GCS, Azure Blob, local filesystem, Parquet\n- CDC for real-time incremental sync; pipeline must be idempotent (dedup on source_id + chunk_hash)\n\n**Data Classification & Knowledge Base Routing:**\n- Tier 1: Auto-pull metadata from existing data catalogs (dbt, DataHub, Apache Atlas, Glue, Snowflake tags)\n- Tier 2: LLM-assisted schema inference for raw databases (sample → classify → admin approves)\n- Tier 3: Column-based routing rules for multi-tenant tables (e.g., WHERE org = 'harvard' → Harvard KB)\n- Admin configures via UI (cloud) or YAML (self-hosted)\n\n**Knowledge Base Vector Storage at Scale (replacing pgvector with ClickHouse + QBit):**\n- Phase 1 (MVP): pgvector + ParadeDB for RAG embeddings (ceiling ~5–10M chunks)\n- Enterprise scale: Migrate embeddings to ClickHouse + QBit, partitioned by org_id/kb_id. PostgreSQL stays for relational data + ParadeDB BM25. Trigger: any tenant exceeds ~5M chunks.\n- Extreme scale (single KB > 10M chunks): Evaluate Qdrant with scalar quantization\n\n**Observability (Two-Tier):**\n- Cloud/enterprise: SigNoz (ClickHouse-backed, all three signals)\n- Edge/self-hosted: OpenObserve (single binary, user-swappable via OTel endpoint env var)\n\n## Edge Optimization (Future)\n\nLow-level Linux kernel optimizations using eBPF. See `docs/research/ebpf-edge-optimization.md`.\n\n**1. XDP Pre-filtering (Rate Limiting Offload)** — Drop/throttle traffic at the NIC before TCP stack.\n**2. Kernel-level Observability (Zero-agent Metrics)** — CPU, memory, syscall metrics via kprobes/tracepoints.\n**3. Security Audit Trail (Process + Syscall Monitoring)** — Trace sys_execve and socket calls for GDPR/SOC2.\n\n## Milestones\n\nSee [GitHub Milestones](https://github.com/ravencloak-org/Raven/milestones) for detailed task tracking."
}
```

---

Now to your question about **GitHub payment integration** — GitHub doesn't have a built-in billing system for charging users for your software. GitHub Sponsors is for donations/patronage, not product licensing. For charging enterprise users, you'd still use **Stripe** (which is already in your M6 roadmap as Task 6.1). GitHub Marketplace exists for selling GitHub Apps, but that's not applicable to Raven's model.

The standard approach for open-core: Stripe handles all billing, your Go backend validates license keys. GitHub is just where the code lives.

Embed Airbyte as the data movement engine for enterprise on-prem/hybrid deployments.

## Scope
- Integrate Airbyte (MIT core) for 700+ source connectors
- Support databases (PostgreSQL, MySQL, MongoDB, ClickHouse, Snowflake, BigQuery)
- Support SaaS sources (Salesforce, HubSpot, Confluence, Google Drive, SharePoint)
- Support file sources (S3, GCS, Azure Blob, Parquet)
- CDC (Change Data Capture) for real-time incremental sync
- Idempotent ingestion pipeline (dedup on source_id + chunk_hash)

## Key Design Decisions
- Airbyte handles data movement; Raven handles chunking, embedding, classification
- YAML config for self-hosted, UI for cloud tier
- See `docs/research/monetization-strategy.md` for tier gating

## Tier: Enterprise
====
Classification system for routing ingested data to correct knowledge bases.

## Three-Tier Approach
1. **Auto-pull metadata** from existing catalogs (Snowflake tags, dbt, DataHub, Apache Atlas, AWS Glue)
2. **LLM-assisted schema inference** for raw databases — sample tables, Claude classifies, admin approves
3. **Column-based routing rules** for multi-tenant tables (e.g., WHERE org='harvard' → Harvard KB)

## Deliverables
- Backend: connector config model with static/column-based/auto routing modes
- Frontend (cloud): mapping UI with tree view, filter rules, catalog browser
- Self-hosted: YAML config equivalent

## Tier: Enterprise (UI), Free (YAML config)
```

The issues capture the high-level decisions but are **missing the detailed tradeoff analysis** we discussed. Specifically:

**Not captured anywhere as a research doc:**
- Why we chose Airbyte over native connectors (the tradeoff analysis)
- Airbyte data integrity risks (type coercion, dedup on retry, schema drift, WAL retention)
- The hybrid deployment model (cloud-with-agent vs on-prem)
- Industry standard data labeling research (Snowflake tags, BigQuery labels, DataHub, Glue — and the finding that none do row-level labeling)
- Why we don't build a labeling system (just consume existing labels or use filter rules)

Let me create the research doc that captures all of this.

> TOOL

tool_use Write
id: toolu_01LKtAeWjGsVNnQri2rvLUFU
```json
{
  "file_path": "/Users/jobinlawrance/Project/raven/docs/research/enterprise-connectors-research.md",
  "content": "# Enterprise Connectors — Research Notes\n\n> Status: **Parked / Future Milestone (Raven Pro)**\n> Assessed: 2026-03-28\n> Related issues: #111, #112, #113, #114, #115, #116\n\n---\n\n## 1. Why Airbyte, Not Native Connectors\n\n### Options Evaluated\n\n| Approach | Pros | Cons |\n|----------|------|------|\n| **Build native connectors** | Full control, lighter weight | Maintaining 20+ connectors long-term (schema changes, API versioning, auth flows per source) |\n| **Embed Airbyte (MIT core)** | 700+ connectors day one, community maintained | Adds dependency, Airbyte platform overhead (~2–4 GB for scheduler/API) |\n| **Hybrid** | Native for databases, Airbyte for SaaS | Two codepaths to maintain |\n\n### Decision: Airbyte for everything\n\nEven for databases (PostgreSQL, MySQL, ClickHouse), Airbyte handles CDC (WAL-based), incremental sync, schema discovery, connection pooling, SSH tunneling, and SSL cert management. Building native Go connectors would start at ~200 lines but grow to 2,000+ per connector. Not worth it.\n\nRaven's value-add is everything **after** Airbyte — chunking, embedding, classification, knowledge graph routing. Not raw data movement.\n\n---\n\n## 2. Airbyte Data Integrity & Tradeoffs\n\n### Initial Sync (Existing Data)\n\nAirbyte does a **Full Refresh** — `SELECT *` from configured tables, streams to destination. Reliable, no corruption risk.\n\n### Known Risks\n\n| Risk | Severity | Mitigation |\n|------|----------|------------|\n| **Type coercion** — JSONB, NUMERIC(38,18) mapped to Airbyte internal types; mismatches logged in `_airbyte_meta.changes` per row | Low | Raven chunks text for embeddings, not doing financial arithmetic |\n| **Duplicate rows on retry** — failed sync + retry, or CDC WAL gap → fallback to full refresh → temporary duplicates until dedup runs | Medium | Idempotent chunking pipeline: `ON CONFLICT (source_id, chunk_hash) DO NOTHING` |\n| **Schema drift** — client alters source table (add/rename/remove columns) between syncs | Medium | Schema mapping in connector config (\"which columns contain text to chunk\") + validation on each sync |\n| **Initial bulk speed** — Airbyte is not the fastest bulk loader vs raw `COPY` | None | Embedding is the bottleneck (orders of magnitude slower than transfer), not Airbyte |\n| **CDC WAL gap** — WAL retention too short + sync interval too long → missed changes → fallback to full refresh | Low | Deployment docs must specify minimum WAL retention per source type |\n\n### Key Requirement\n\n**Raven's chunking/embedding pipeline must be idempotent.** Dedup on `(source_id, chunk_hash)`. This handles retry duplicates, full refresh fallbacks, and CDC gaps.\n\n---\n\n## 3. Deployment Models\n\n### Option A: Cloud-with-agent (mid-market)\n\n```\n[Client's Postgres / S3 / Salesforce]\n         ↓ (Airbyte agent on client network, secure tunnel)\n[Raven Cloud — Airbyte destination]\n         ↓\n[Raven ingestion pipeline — chunking, embedding, classification]\n         ↓\n[Knowledge base (pgvector / ClickHouse + QBit)]\n```\n\n### Option B: On-premise (enterprise)\n\n```\n[Client's internal data sources]\n         ↓ (Airbyte running inside client infra)\n[Raven on-prem — full stack inside client network]\n         ↓\n[Knowledge base — data never leaves client network]\n```\n\n### Decision: Support both (Option C)\n\nEnterprise gets on-prem. Mid-market gets cloud-with-agent. Same Raven codebase, different deployment config.\n\n---\n\n## 4. Data Classification & Knowledge Base Routing\n\n### Industry Standard Research\n\nEnterprise data systems have labeling, but at different granularities:\n\n| System | Labels | Granularity | Row-level? |\n|--------|--------|-------------|------------|\n| **Snowflake** | Tags (key-value, inherited) | Database → schema → table → column | No |\n| **BigQuery** | Labels (key-value) | Dataset → table → view | No |\n| **AWS Glue** | Data Catalog (auto-crawled) | Database → table → column | No |\n| **DataHub** | Tags + Business Glossary | Dataset → schema → column | No |\n| **dbt** | Tags + meta + descriptions | Model → column | No |\n\n**Key finding:** No system labels at the row level. They tell you \"this table is owned by Marketing\" but not \"rows 1–50K belong to Institution A.\"\n\n### The Two Classification Problems\n\n**Problem 1: Which tables/sources feed which knowledge base?**\nStructural mapping. Snowflake tags, dbt metadata, DataHub terms ARE useful here. If the enterprise already tagged their data, Raven reads tags and auto-routes.\n\n**Problem 2: Within a single table, which rows go where?**\nMulti-tenant table scenario. No external labeling system solves this — it's a filter condition. Admin tells Raven: \"From `documents` table, rows where `org = 'harvard'` → Harvard KB.\"\n\n### Decision: Don't Build a Labeling System\n\nRaven consumes labels that already exist, or lets the admin point at what they want exposed.\n\n**Three routing modes:**\n\n```yaml\n# Mode 1: Static — whole table → one KB\n- name: support_tickets\n  knowledge_base: \"Support KB\"\n  text_columns: [subject, body, resolution]\n\n# Mode 2: Column-based — route by discriminator column\n- name: documents\n  text_columns: [title, content]\n  routing:\n    column: institution\n    rules:\n      - value: \"harvard\"\n        knowledge_base: \"Harvard KB\"\n      - value: \"mit\"\n        knowledge_base: \"MIT KB\"\n      - default: \"Unclassified KB\"\n\n# Mode 3: Auto — LLM classifies on ingest (expensive, use sparingly)\n- name: product_catalog\n  knowledge_base: auto\n  text_columns: [name, description, specs]\n```\n\n**When external catalogs exist** (Snowflake tags, dbt, DataHub), Raven pre-populates this config by reading the tags. Admin confirms rather than building from scratch.\n\n### UI vs Config\n\n- **Cloud tier:** Mapping UI (tree view, filter rules, drag-drop tagging)\n- **Self-hosted:** YAML config file (if they can self-host, they can write YAML)\n- Both are paid features in the `ee/` directory\n\n---\n\n## 5. Architecture Flow\n\n```\n[Client Data Source]\n         ↓\n[Airbyte (sync)]\n         ↓\n[Raven Connector Service]\n    ├── Schema validation (detect drift)\n    ├── Apply routing rules (static / column-based / auto)\n    ├── Extract text columns\n    └── Dedup check (source_id + chunk_hash)\n         ↓\n[Raven Ingestion Pipeline (existing)]\n    ├── Chunking\n    ├── Embedding (BYOK LLM)\n    └── Store in knowledge base\n         ↓\n[pgvector (MVP) / ClickHouse + QBit (enterprise scale)]\n```\n"
}
```

{
  "command": "gh issue edit 111 --body \"Embed Airbyte as the data movement engine for enterprise on-prem/hybrid deployments.\n\n## Scope\n- Integrate Airbyte (MIT core) for 700+ source connectors\n- Support databases (PostgreSQL, MySQL, MongoDB, ClickHouse, Snowflake, BigQuery)\n- Support SaaS sources (Salesforce, HubSpot, Confluence, Google Drive, SharePoint)\n- Support file sources (S3, GCS, Azure Blob, Parquet)\n- CDC (Change Data Capture) for real-time incremental sync\n- Idempotent ingestion pipeline (dedup on source_id + chunk_hash)\n\n## Deployment Models\n- **Cloud-with-agent:** Raven cloud + secure Airbyte agent on client network\n- **On-premise:** Full Airbyte + Raven stack inside client infrastructure\n\n## Key Design Decisions\n- Airbyte handles all data movement (even databases) — not building native connectors\n- Raven handles post-Airbyte: chunking, embedding, classification, KB routing\n- YAML config for self-hosted, UI for cloud tier\n- Pipeline must be idempotent: dedup on (source_id, chunk_hash) to handle retries and CDC gaps\n\n## Data Integrity Notes\n- Type coercion: low risk (we chunk text, not financial data)\n- Schema drift: connector config must specify text columns; validate on each sync\n- WAL retention: deployment docs must specify minimum per source type\n\n## Research\nSee \\`docs/research/enterprise-connectors-research.md\\` for full tradeoff analysis (why Airbyte over native, data integrity risks, deployment models)\n\n## Tier: Enterprise\" && gh issue edit 112 --body \"Classification system for routing ingested data to correct knowledge bases.\n\n## Industry Context\nNo enterprise data system (Snowflake, BigQuery, DataHub, dbt, AWS Glue) does row-level labeling. They label at table/column level only. Multi-tenant tables require filter conditions, not labels.\n\n**Decision: Don't build a labeling system.** Consume existing labels or let admins define routing rules.\n\n## Three-Tier Approach\n1. **Auto-pull metadata** from existing catalogs (Snowflake tags, dbt, DataHub, Apache Atlas, AWS Glue) — zero admin effort\n2. **LLM-assisted schema inference** for raw databases — sample tables, Claude classifies, admin approves\n3. **Column-based routing rules** for multi-tenant tables (e.g., WHERE org='harvard' → Harvard KB)\n\n## Three Routing Modes\n- **Static:** whole table → one KB (most common)\n- **Column-based:** route by discriminator column value (multi-tenant case)\n- **Auto:** LLM classifies each row during ingestion (expensive, use sparingly)\n\n## Deliverables\n- Backend: connector config model with static/column-based/auto routing modes\n- Frontend (cloud): mapping UI with tree view, filter rules, catalog browser\n- Self-hosted: YAML config equivalent\n\n## Research\nSee \\`docs/research/enterprise-connectors-research.md\\` for full analysis (industry labeling standards, why no custom labeling system, routing mode design)\n\n## Tier: Enterprise (UI + catalog integration), Free (YAML config for self-hosted)\"",
  "description": "Update issues #111 and #112 with full research references"
}

> AGENT

Now, here are your real options:

## Payment Gateways That Work From India + Accept Global Payments

| Gateway | Domestic (UPI/RuPay) | International (Visa/MC) | Subscriptions | Usage-based billing | License key integration |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Razorpay** | UPI, RuPay, netbanking, wallets | Visa, Mastercard, PayPal, international cards | Yes (Razorpay Subscriptions API) | Yes (usage-based add-ons) | Webhook → generate key |
| **Cashfree** | UPI, RuPay, netbanking | Visa, Mastercard | Yes | Limited | Webhook → generate key |
| **Paddle** (MoR) | No domestic Indian | Visa, MC, PayPal, Apple Pay | Yes | Yes | Webhook → generate key |
| **LemonSqueezy** (MoR) | No domestic Indian | Visa, MC, PayPal | Yes | Yes (2024+) | Built-in license key generation |

## The Two Realistic Options

**Option 1: Razorpay (recommended)**

The Razorpay of Indian SaaS. Handles both sides:
- **Domestic:** UPI, RuPay, netbanking, wallets — Indian customers pay in INR
- **International:** Visa, Mastercard, international cards — global customers pay in USD/EUR
- **Subscriptions API** — handles recurring billing, plan upgrades/downgrades, invoices
- **Webhooks** — `payment.captured`, `subscription.activated`, etc. — same pattern as Stripe
- **Settlement** — directly to your Indian bank account
- **Go SDK** — `github.com/razorpay/razorpay-go`

The API is very similar to Stripe. Your existing Task 6.1 (Stripe Integration) would just become "Razorpay Integration" — same architecture, different SDK.

**Option 2: Razorpay (domestic) + Paddle (international)**

If you want to avoid dealing with international tax compliance (GST on exports, US sales tax, EU VAT):
- **Paddle** is a Merchant of Record — they handle global taxes, billing, and remit to you. You're selling *through* Paddle, not directly.
- **Razorpay** for Indian customers who prefer UPI/RuPay
- Downside: two payment systems to integrate and reconcile

## My Recommendation

**Start with Razorpay only.** It handles both domestic and international. The API is mature, Go SDK exists, and you avoid the complexity of two billing systems. If international tax compliance becomes a headache later (it will if you get 50+ international customers), add Paddle as the international MoR and keep Razorpay for domestic.

One thing to note — **LemonSqueezy has built-in license key generation** which maps perfectly to your `ee/` licensing model. But it doesn't support Indian domestic payments. Worth considering as the international-only gateway in the Razorpay + international split if you go that route later.

## Hyperswitch supported payment gateways connectors list

--- [Hyperswitch GitHub] ---
### Composable Open-Source Payments Infrastructure > Quickstart > Hosted Sandbox (No Setup Required)

### Hosted Sandbox (No Setup Required)

[](#hosted-sandbox-no-setup-required)

Hyperswitch offers a fully hosted sandbox environment that requires no setup. You can explore the Control Center, configure payment connectors, and test payments directly from the UI.

 [![](https://github.com/juspay/hyperswitch/raw/main/docs/imgs/try-the-sandbox.png?raw=true)](https://app.hyperswitch.io)**What you can do in the Hosted Sandbox**

*   Access the full Control Center
*   Configure payment connectors
*   View logs, routing rules, and retry strategies
*   Try payments directly from the UI

--- [Hyperswitch GitHub] ---
### Composable Open-Source Payments Infrastructure > Quickstart > Local Setup via Docker

### Local Setup via Docker

[](#-local-setup-via-docker-)

```shell
# One-click local setup

git clone --depth 1 --branch latest https://github.com/juspay/hyperswitch

cd hyperswitch

scripts/setup.sh
```

**This script:**

*   Detects Docker/Podman
*   Offers multiple deployment profiles:
    *   **Standard**: App server + Control Center
    *   **Full**: Includes monitoring + schedulers
    *   **Minimal**: Standalone App server
*   Provides access links when done

If you need further help, check out our [video tutorial](https://docs.hyperswitch.io/hyperswitch-open-source/overview/unified-local-setup-using-docker).

👉 After setup, [configure a connector](https://docs.hyperswitch.io/hyperswitch-open-source/account-setup/using-hyperswitch-control-center#add-a-payment-processor) and [test a payment](https://docs.hyperswitch.io/hyperswitch-open-source/account-setup/test-a-payment).

## Hyperswitch smart routing failover retry

--- [Hyperswitch GitHub] ---
### Composable Open-Source Payments Infrastructure > Why Hyperswitch?

## Why Hyperswitch?

[](#why-hyperswitch)

Hyperswitch is a commercial open-source payments stack purpose-built for scale, flexibility, and developer experience. Designed with a modular architecture, Hyperswitch lets you pick only the components you need—whether it’s routing, retries, vaulting, or observability—without vendor lock-in or bloated integrations.

Built in Rust for performance and reliability, Hyperswitch supports global payment methods (cards, wallets, BNPL, UPI, Pay by Bank), exposes smart routing and retry logic, and provides a visual workflow builder in the Control Center. Whether you're integrating a full payment suite or augmenting an existing stack with a single module, Hyperswitch meets you where you are.

**“Linux for Payments”** — Hyperswitch is a well-architected reference for teams who want to own their payments stack.

We believe in:

*   **Embracing Payment Diversity:** Innovation comes from enabling choice—across payment methods, processors, and flows.
    
*   **Open Source by Default:** Transparency drives trust and bu…

…aped by real-world use cases and contributors.
    
*   **Systems-Level Engineering:** We hold ourselves to a high bar for reliability, security, and performance.
    
*   **Maximizing Value Creation:** For developers, customers, and partners alike.
    
*   **Community-Driven, Enterprise-Tested:** Hyperswitch is built in the open with real-world feedback from developers and contributors, and maintained by Juspay, the team powering p

--- [Hyperswitch GitHub] ---
### Composable Open-Source Payments Infrastructure > What Can I Do with Hyperswitch? > Learn More About The Payment Modules

…

[](#-learn-more-about-the-payment-modules-)

Details

*   **Cost Observability**  
    Advanced observability tools to audit, monitor, and optimize your payment costs. Detect hidden fees, downgrades, and penalties with self-serve dashboards and actionable insights.  
    _[Read more](https://docs.hyperswitch.io/about-hyperswitch/payments-modules/ai-powered-cost-observability)_
    
*   **Revenue Recovery**  
    Combat passive churn with intelligent retry strategies tuned by card bin, region, method, and more. Offers fine-grained control over retry algorithms, penalty budgets, and recovery transparency.  
    _[Read more](https://docs.hyperswitch.io/about-hyperswitch/payments-modules/revenue-recovery)_
    
*   **Vault**  
    A PCI-compliant vault service to store cards, tokens, wallets, and bank credentials. Provides a unified, secure, and reusable store of customer-linked payment methods.  
    _[Read more](https://docs.hyperswitch.io/about-hyperswitch/payments-modules/vault)_
    
*   **Intelligent Routing**  
    Route each transaction to the PSP with the highest predicted auth rate. Reduce retries, avoid downtime, and minimize latency while maximizing first attempt success.  
    _[Read more](https://docs.hyperswitch.io/about-hyperswitch/payments-modules/intelligent-routing)_
    
*   **Reconciliation**  
    Automate 2-way and 3-way reconciliation with backdated support, staggered scheduling, and customizable outputs. Reduces manual ops effort and increases audit con

## What Can I Do with Hyperswitch?

[](#-what-can-i-do-with-hyperswitch)

Hyperswitch offers a modular, open-source payments infrastructure designed for flexibility and control. Apart from our Payment Suite offering, this solution allows businesses to pick and integrate only the modules they need on top of their existing payment stack — without unnecessary complexity or vendor lock-in.

Each module is independent and purpose-built to optimize different aspects of payment processing.

> AGENT

This is exactly what you need. **Hyperswitch** by Juspay — and it's an Indian company too.

## Hyperswitch — Open-Source Payment Orchestration

**Repository:** [github.com/juspay/hyperswitch](https://github.com/juspay/hyperswitch) — 41.8K stars, Apache 2.0, written in Rust

Built by **Juspay** (the company behind Google Pay India's payment processing). This does exactly what you described:

| Feature | What it does |
|---|---|
| **Multi-gateway routing** | Connect Razorpay, Stripe, Paddle, Adyen, PayPal, 70+ gateways simultaneously |
| **Smart routing** | Route to cheapest gateway, or highest success rate, or by region/currency |
| **Automatic failover** | If Razorpay is down → automatically retry via Stripe/Paddle — zero downtime |
| **Cost observability** | Dashboard showing per-gateway fees, hidden charges, downgrades |
| **Revenue recovery** | Intelligent retry on failed payments (tuned by card type, region, method) |
| **PCI-compliant vault** | Store card tokens once, use across all gateways |
| **UPI + international** | Supports UPI, RuPay, BNPL, wallets, cards — all methods via one API |
| **Self-hostable** | Docker one-click setup, or deploy on AWS/GCP/Azure via Helm |

**How it changes the architecture:**

```
Your Raven app (Go)
    → Hyperswitch API (single integration)
        → Razorpay (domestic INR, UPI, RuPay)
        → Paddle (international, MoR for tax)
        → Stripe (if you ever get access)
        → LemonSqueezy (license keys)
    ← Unified webhooks back to Raven
```

You write **one** payment integration against Hyperswitch's API. Hyperswitch routes to the optimal gateway based on your rules. If one goes down, it fails over automatically.

**This is a better answer than "just use Razorpay."** It gives you gateway independence from day one. Want me to update Task 6.1 and the monetization doc to use Hyperswitch as the payment orchestration layer?

on subscription plan)\n- License key generation for ee/ features triggered on subscription activation\n\n## Why Hyperswitch\n- Open-source (Apache 2.0), 41.8K GitHub stars, built in Rust\n- Built by Razorpay handles domestic India (UPI, RuPay, netbanking), Paddle handles international (Visa/MC + Merchant of Record for tax compliance). Additional gateways can be added via Hyperswitch connectors without code changes."
